## Purpose

The Arkansas Wildlife Action Plan serves as a cooperative, strategic framework for conserving the state's native species and habitats. By identifying and prioritizing the most at-risk species and outlining key conservation actions, it ensures a targeted and effective approach to preserving Arkansas's biodiversity.

## Foreword

Chapter 1: Introduction Guide to 8 Required Elements Congress identified eight required elements to be addressed in these wildlife conservation plans. Further, the plan must identify and focus on the "species in greatest need of conservation," yet address the "full array of wildlife" and wildlife-related issues. They must provide and make use of:
1) SPECIES: The distribution and abundance of species of wildlife, including low and declining populations as each State fish and wildlife agency deems appropriate, that are indicative of the diversity and health of the State's Species of Greatest Conservation Need (SGCN).
2) HABITATS: The location and relative condition of key habitats and community types essential to the conservation of each State's SGCN.
3) THREATS/STRESSORS: The problems that may adversely affect SGCN or their habitats, and priority research and surveys needed to identify factors that may assist in restoration and improved conservation of SGCN and their habitats.

## 4) ACTIONS:

The actions necessary to conserve SGCN and their habitats and the priorities for implementing such conservation actions.

## 5) MONITORING:

The provisions for periodic monitoring of SGCN and their habitats, for monitoring the effectiveness of conservation actions, and for adapting conservation actions as appropriate to respond to new information or changing conditions.
6) REVIEW AND REVISION: Each State's provisions to review its Plan at intervals not to exceed 10 years.
7) PARTNER INVOLVEMENT: Each State's provisions for coordination during the development, implementation, review, and revision of its Plan with Federal, State, and local agencies and Indian Tribes that manage significant areas of land or water within the State, or administer programs that significantly affect the conservation of species or their habitats.
8) PUBLIC INVOLVEMENT: Each State's provisions to provide the necessary public participation in the development, revision, and implementation of the Plan.

## Roadmap to 8 Required Elements

A Collaborative, Data-Driven Approach to Conservation
The Arkansas Wildlife Action Plan (AWAP) was designed from the beginning to be a living planning tool, with the goal of creating an invaluable resource for everyone interested or involved in conservation, from professional partners and land managers to researchers and citizen conservationists. At its core is a dynamic database, populated by experts and professionals, that stores and links crucial information. This database allows us to calculate conservation priorities and can be easily updated as new data becomes available or conservation actions are completed.
This approach ensures that the Plan remains current, and that with every update, we can efficiently reexamine the relationships between species, habitats, threats, and conservation actions, and clearly demonstrate progress over time. Our mission is to work collaboratively with partners and the public, focusing our available resources to reach a common conservation goal.
We will accomplish this goal by utilizing all available information to make science-based decisions, and by making that information accessible and usable for everyone involved in conservation and land management across Arkansas.
In light of the unparalleled threats and profound impacts our wildlife and ecosystems currently endure, the traditional pursuit of knowledge, while inherently valuable, must now be strategically reoriented. It is our hope that this plan and the needed actions and research identified within will be used as a reference and a guide to not only direct conservation actions in the state, but also that the research needs, or data gaps, identified here will be prioritized by all to help remove existing conservation barriers. Future research endeavors, supported by available funding, must be singularly focused on addressing the specific data gaps and implementing the direct solutions outlined herein. The severe losses that our native wildlife and habitats have already suffered, continue to be exacerbated by ever growing and compounding threats, which will only continue to worsen without significant reform and additional dedicated resources to address the issues.
If we wish to protect and conserve these species, ecosystems, and Arkansas's amazing biodiversity, we must focus all available resources and energy on identifying and addressing obstacles to conservation efforts and implementing those actions and protections to ensure the long-term conservation of these species and their habitats. To effectively protect and conserve Arkansas's irreplaceable biodiversity, we must collectively dedicate all available resources and energy into actionable research that directly informs and accelerates on-the-ground conservation.
Monitoring and adaptive management are also key elements of the conservation effort.
Agencies and partners cannot afford to undertake large scale habitat protection, restoration or enhancement endeavors, only to discover after years of management that actions were ineffective or even counterproductive. Monitoring helps evaluate species, population, and habitat responses to conservation actions, progress toward population and habitat objectives, and test assumptions made in species-habitat models and decision support tools.
New information generated from research and monitoring only becomes useful if it influences future conservation decisions and actions. These benefits are most pronounced when the efforts are iterative and ongoing, rather than static or episodic. Thus, species and habitat conservation strategies are most appropriately viewed as living strategies that are continually developing in response to targeted research and monitoring results. A long-term view is required because effects on target species may be difficult to measure or may not be noticeable for years after the conservation action was taken. Successful implementation of the Plan will depend upon sharing and incorporating new information into the overall body of knowledge held by the AWAP, and may be measured by tracking changes in SGCN priority tier or conservation status ranks and through the number of priority data gaps or conservation actions still needing to be addressed.

## Implementing Arkansas's Wildlife Action Plan

Problems faced by Arkansas's wildlife and ecosystems are many and varied. They include the destruction, conversion, and fragmentation of habitats, pollution, aquatic barriers, sedimentation, climate change, as well as the advance of invasive plant and animal species, diseases, and pathogens.
Given the current limits to available resources and funding, doing our best for species of greatest conservation need means that efforts must be targeted with an eye to optimizing results.
Because there is much more to do to conserve SGCN than can be funded in a given year, a science-based and strategic prioritization process will be implemented to make the most efficient use of available funds and resources. The process will rely on a database framework for organizing, analyzing, storing, and retrieving data, and it will rely on input from biologists, land managers, conservation organizations, government agencies, researchers, the general public, and the many partners whose involvement and contribution have been invaluable in the development and implantation of the AWAP.
This database is the foundation of Arkansas's systematic approach to identifying and ranking implementation needs. The database will be continuously updated as new information becomes available or as conservation actions or data gaps are completed or addressed. This ensures an effective and efficient, up-to-date system to track and prioritize needs and guide the allocation of funding and resources.
Priority conservation needs will be identified from the database and will be determined through a combination of factors, such as SGCN priority tier and conservation status, how many SGCN will benefit, urgency, project design, feasibility, and cost, funding availability, partnership/leverage opportunities, and pending federal listings or determinations. Each step in the process will receive expert input from the Plan's partners and stakeholders, to promote efficient, collaborative, and science-based decisions, supporting activities providing the greatest benefit to Arkansas's SGCN and their habitats.
The current primary method for implementing the AWAP is through the State Wildlife Grants (SWG) program is a non-competitive federal grant program that is managed by the United States Fish and Wildlife Service's (USFWS) office of Conservation Investment (CI), formerly known as Wildlife and Sportfish Restoration (WSFR). Funds are annually appropriated by Congress and allocated to states based on a formula that considers each state's land area and population size.
Additionally, each year a portion of the funding is set aside for Competitive State Wildlife Grants (C-SWG) that encourage multi-state conservation efforts.
State Wildlife Grants are awarded to state wildlife agencies to implement their USFWS approved State Wildlife Action Plans (SWAP). These funds must be used to address the conservation needs of Species of Greatest Conservation Need (SGCN) identified in each state's Wildlife Action Plan.
State Wildlife Grants have a 35% cost share or match requirement to leverage the federal dollars and promote shared investment in conservation. SWG funding will continue to be used to fund the highest priority conservation needs. An important note, even with the inclusion of plant species on the new SGCN list, plants are currently not eligible to receive SWG funding. Additional efforts will be made to attempt to secure additional funding and to establish alternative, dedicated funding sources to better implement the AWAP.

## Public and Partner Engagement and Involvement

Since the development of the first version of the Arkansas Wildlife Action Plan, Arkansas has continued to foster scientific community and public involvement in planning and implementation in a number of ways. Every opportunity to relay information on species of greatest conservation need to the public is taken.
Engaging the Public Presentations on SGCN have been given to school groups, college classes, master naturalist groups, podcasts, and public outreach events. Success stories for SGCN as a result of state wildlife grant projects and other efforts are disseminated as often as possible, typically through AGFC's weekly Arkansas Outdoors newsletter and on social media platforms or through featured articles in other state outdoor and wildlife magazines.
Additionally, various state land managers that assist private landowners in developing wildlife management plans for their properties, such as the Arkansas Game and Fish Commission (AGFC)
Private Lands Division, the Arkansas Chapter of Quail Forever's Farm Bill biologists, and others, utilize the AWAP as a reference guide benefit SGCN and prioritize habitat work.

## Engaging the Scientific Community

Presentations on the AWAP, SGCN, and project updates have also been given at science conferences, workshops, and partner meetings. The scientific community has been heavily involved in the implementation of the Plan. This includes various organizations and universities receiving state wildlife grants to implement conservation actions or address data gaps to providing species and habitat information and serving on committees and taxa teams to determine funding priorities.
The central involvement of partners in the development of the Arkansas Wildlife Action Plan has resulted in stronger partnerships and increased collaborations and support for implementing the Plan. Furthermore, input from the scientific community is requested on all state wildlife grant project proposals. Project results and performance measures will be reported to AWAP partners and stakeholders at least biennially.

## Biennial Arkansas Wildlife Action Plan Symposium

Since 2006, an Arkansas Wildlife Action Plan Symposium has been held every 2 years to bring partners together for collaboration. At the Symposium, results from state wildlife grant funded projects are presented. Also, the taxa teams and habitat teams meet to discuss and determine the top needs for species and habitats. They select the highest conservation actions, research, and monitoring priorities to be funded with state wildlife grant dollars. Attendance at the symposium has increased each time and a number of partners from state and federal agencies, non-governmental organizations, and universities are represented.

## Revision Process and Schedule

A formal, comprehensive review of each state's State Wildlife Action Plan (SWAP) is required every ten years by the USFWS. Arkansas formally notified the USFWS of its intent to begin the second comprehensive review and revision of the AWAP in June, 2023.
The main objective of this revision was to streamline the plan, while improving its utility, by providing all the necessary information to guide conservation actions for these species and their habitats in a more condensed and effective format. A second objective was to incorporate new information and recommendations from national and regional efforts to standardize and improve SWAPs. To accomplish these objectives, we decided to conduct a complete overhaul of the Plan.
The revision process began by AGFC staff and partners reviewing and updating species state conservation status ranks (S-ranks). AGFC staff then started developing a new database to amass, store and organize the vast amount of information, described here within, which is necessary to develop and implement the Plan. All major partners were invited to participate in the review and revision of the Plan. Many partners participated by serving on specific taxa and habitat review teams and were instrumental in the updating of the Plan. These inter-agency teams worked to review, update, and transition the species and habitat information from the previous database into the new standardized format. See Chapter 2, Chapter 3, and Chapter 4 for discussions on how the specific parts of the AWAP were updated and for how the new components were integrated.
In summary, the re-evaluation of species' S-ranks, the addition of plants to list, and the adoption of the new priority tier method to determining and prioritizing SGCN resulted in the addition of several new species to the list of species of greatest conservation need, as well as the removal of some species, for various reasons. Once species' S-ranks were updated, taxa teams proposed a list of species for consideration to be for SGCN status. The necessary additional information was compiled for each of these species, and using the newly adopted reference criteria, the proposed species were sorted into five tiers (Highest, High, Moderate, Data Deficient, Not SGCN). Taxa teams then reviewed the sorted lists and submitted requests to recategorize species that they felt belonged in a different priority tier based on available information and their expert opinion.
Once the new SGCN list was finalized, taxa teams began to identify threats, conservation actions, data gaps, habitat associations, distribution, and other information to populate the new database. This information was then used to develop the new, condensed species profiles.
Distribution maps were updated with the most current spatial data from multiple sources.
The terrestrial and aquatic habitat teams began by reviewing and assessing the previous habitat lists. After reviewing the lists, habitat teams decided to update a few of the habitat types to better align with NatureServe Explorer's Ecological Systems and Arkansas's approach to habitat classification and management. The updated habitat classification system for this revision, Release was distributed on August 4, 2025, with a link to the document, a survey, and an email address for additional comments, requesting their review and feedback on the draft plan. A thirty-day review period was given. Arkansas does not have any federally recognized Native American tribes, so no specific contacts were made to this group. All feedback will be reviewed and considered for incorporation into the final Plan.

## Plan Submission and Distribution

The revised plan will be submitted to the USFWS by October 1, 2025. Upon plan approval, the AGFC will provide all conservation partners and the public at large with access to the updated Plan via the Arkansas Game and Fish Commission AWAP webpage.
To further improve accessibility and utilization of the Plan, we will begin developing an online, interactive dashboard, to transition from the traditional paper document style plan into a more user-friendly and accessible digital format by the next revision.

## Commitment to Periodic Review and Revision

AGFC commits to completing a third comprehensive review and revision of the AWAP process and plan by October 1, 2035. At that time, not only will the functional process be evaluated, but the database, protocols and fundamental logic behind assumptions will be reassessed. We anticipate following a similar process in updating the SGCN list, SGCN status and information, and the habitat lists and associated management and restoration recommendations.
Chapter 2: Species of Greatest Conservation Need Introduction Species of Greatest Conservation Need (SGCN) are native plant and animal species that are considered or are thought to be in decline and are at a greater risk of extirpation or even extinction. These species require proactive efforts, such as specific conservation actions, additional research to address data gaps, or monitoring efforts, to prevent further declines and ensure their long-term survival and to preclude potential federal listing under the Endangered Species Act (ESA). The reason for the decline of species may be due to a single factor or a combination of factors, having a synergistic effect on these species and their populations. This may include, but is not limited to, the rarity of the species, the number or severity of threats a species or their habitat faces, limited habitat availability or distribution, impacts to particular stages of their life history, competition with or predation by invasive species, taxonomic uncertainty, or limited knowledge or understanding of their life history, requirements, or reason for their decline. Some species may have very specific habitat requirements, a narrow-range, extreme vulnerability to environmental changes, limited mobility, low reproduction or survival rates, or a small population size.
Given the extreme and often compounding threats that these species and their habitats face and the extensive work and considerable resources that are necessary to address those threats (which are often limited), a targeted and efficient use of available resources and funding is extremely important. The establishment of a list of Species of Greatest Conservation Need is the critical first step in prioritizing and guiding these efforts to conserve biodiversity and protect our most imperiled and at-risk species. This enables us to better focus conservation efforts to address and effectively manage the overall health of ecosystems at a landscape-level, through a process to identify species or groups of species that will be the focus of programs and projects supported by federal funding via the State Wildlife Grant (SWG) program along with other available funding sources.
It is also worth clarifying that the creation of this list is not to say that these species are or should be our only focus or that the species on this list are the only at-risk species in the state, but instead should be considered a broad representation of the diverse taxa of Arkansas, for which the conservation of these species would also benefit other species inhabiting those same ecosystems.

## SGCN List Original Development and First Revision

## SGCN List

Arkansas's first SGCN list was established in 2005 during the initial development of the Arkansas Wildlife Action Plan (AWAP). To accomplish this task, the AWAP Species Team (or Taxa Teams) assembled a list of potential species from the existing lists of rare, declining, or imperiled fauna kept by the Arkansas Natural Heritage Commission and the Arkansas Game and Fish Commission.
Existing data from agencies and partners was cross-referenced with expert opinion. The team decided to consider all native mammals, birds, reptiles, amphibians, and fish for inclusion on the list. Of the invertebrates, all native crayfish and mussels were considered for the list. Only representative insects and other invertebrates were considered because the team was concerned that the numbers of these species, many with poorly known conservation status, could overwhelm the list. Furthermore, some species were chosen for inclusion on the list because not enough was known about their life history or taxonomic status. Undescribed species and species with apparently more secure statuses (G4-G5 and S4-S5) were also included for consideration if their populations were thought to be in decline or if little was known about their conservation status. Species removed from consideration were those that were thought to be extirpated or extinct or those that are common elsewhere in their range but rare in Arkansas because the state is on the periphery of their range.
To identify SGCN and to best prioritize the efforts that would be directed by the AWAP, the team developed a protocol to evaluate all of the proposed species and to set a threshold for listing those species as SGCN. An assigned 'Priority Score', or degree of imperilment, for each proposed SGCN allows the teams to be consistent when determining SGCN status and provides a means to prioritize the conservation efforts listed within the plan. This helps to guide limited resources to better address the most pressing needs of species, or groups of species, included in the AWAP database. Priority scores were calculated using NatureServe conservation ranks and population trends. NatureServe conservation ranks use a 1-5 scale to assess the rarity and risk of extinction for species and ecosystems, with 1 being the most critically imperiled and 5 being secure. These ranks are assigned globally (G ranks) and at the state or subnational level (S ranks). Additional qualifiers and letters can further refine these ranks to indicate uncertainty or specific characteristics. Population trends were determined by the taxa teams using information derived from literature reviews, expert opinion or recent survey data. The first version of the AWAP ultimately listed 369 species of greatest conservation need.

## SGCN List

For the first (2015) revision of the list, taxa teams reviewed and updated state ranks for several species in various taxa groups. The priority scores were then calculated or updated for any newly proposed species or SGCN that had a change in G-rank, S-rank, or population trend. Priority score thresholds were set by each taxa team to help determine which species would be considered SGCN. This resulted in the addition of 66 species due to increased priority and deletion of 57 species due to increased information and lowering of their priority score. The number of SGCN increased to 377, followed by a minor revision in 2019 to add three additional species and bringing the new total to 380 species. and 'Data Deficient' (Data Deficient) and are based on predetermined reference criteria (Table 
2
.1). For many species, not enough is known about their status, distribution, taxonomic relationships, life history and ecological relationships to develop an approach to conservation. In some cases, basic research or status surveys are required before appropriate conservation actions or monitoring strategies can be prescribed. Furthermore, these are species that are thought to warrant SGCN status, but additional information is necessary to confirm their true status, so they are identified as 'Data Deficient' SGCN.

## SGCN List Revision

The categorization of SGCN into these tiers helps to determine which species are considered to be the highest priority and require the greatest or most immediate attention. The reference criteria listed in the table below have been slightly modified from the original recommendation, but includes NatureServe Global (G-rank) and State (S-rank) Conservation Status Ranks, Regional SGCN (R-SGCN) status, federal listing status, and Partners in Flight (PIF) status (for birds only). Species that may be imperiled, but lack of information on their conservation status, taxonomy, life history, and threats precludes the identification of conservation actions.
G3-G5 or T3-T5 and SU or SNR or SX* or SH* (*Species that may be rediscovered or reintroduced)
** The priority tier of some SGCN may not meet these criteria. Taxa team leads were given the flexibility to reassign tiers based on expert opinion and the availability of additional information that is not captured here.

## Taxa Team Reviews and Contributions

The initial approach to updating the SGCN list remained largely the same. The review process began by using the current SGCN list as the starting point, which was divided up by faunal associations, or into Taxa Teams: Mammals, Birds, Reptiles, Amphibians, Fish, Crayfish, Mussels, Karst (Invertebrates), and Insects and Other Invertebrates. In addition to these, a new Plant Taxa Team was established, which worked to develop the first Plant SGCN list. These Taxa Teams consist of experts drawn from a coalition of public agencies, private nonprofit organizations, and academic institutions. Taxa teams were then tasked with developing a list of species, for their respective group, that they felt warranted consideration for SGCN status. These lists were developed based on available information and data and the team's collective experience, knowledge, and expert opinions.
Similar to the last revision, taxa teams began this cycle by reviewing and updating S-ranks for species where new species or population information has become available, or for those that have been or may be impacted by new or emerging threats. Taxa Teams evaluated their respective SGCN list and reviewed any newly available information obtained from State Wildlife Grant projects, ESA Section 6 projects, AGFC staff and partners' research and monitoring projects and data collections, occurrence data from eBird and iNaturalist, and any other available resources to determine which S-ranks needed to be updated. This included both species that were and were not currently SGCN.
Along with species that were determined to have high G-or S-ranks, taxa teams included additional species on their proposed lists for various other reasons. This included, but was not limited to, species that underwent a recent taxonomic split, whose current conservation status is unknown, whose population is thought to be declining, or those that may be considered 'keystone', 'indicator', 'umbrella', or 'flagship' species. Inclusion of these types of species on the list facilitates a more efficient approach to the conservation and management of similar species or those that have similar habitat requirements by keeping the SGCN list at an effective and manageable size.

## Process for Updating and Finalizing the 2025 SGCN List

Once taxa teams compiled their lists, the proposed species were sorted into one of five tiers based on the reference criteria identified in table 2.1. These tiers are 'Highest', 'High', 'Moderate', 'Data Deficient', or 'Not SGCN', if the proposed species did not meet the reference criteria for one of the four SGCN or priority tiers. Taxa teams were then given the opportunity to review the categorization of their proposed species and, with sufficient justification, recategorize them into a different tier, which more accurately reflects their conservation status or priority within the state. Species were recategorized into different tiers for a variety of reasons, such as a species having an outdated or inaccurate G-rank or S-rank, or the classification of a species on one of the referenced lists (R-SGCN, PIF) that is more representative of their status in a different portion of their range, which artificially skewed their priority tier; conservation opportunities for said species in Arkansas are limited or not feasible, based on their life history, specific threats or requirements, limited distribution in the state, etc.; recent taxonomic changes; or species is now presumed to be extirpated or extinct. This new system of determining SGCN status resulted in 520 Species of Greatest Conservation Need (Table 
2
.2).
It is important to note that a species inclusion on the list of Species of Greatest Conservation Need does not confer any special or regulatory status or protections. Federally-listed species that occur in Arkansas are included on the SGCN list. Arkansas Game and Fish Commission's list of state endangered species largely mirrors the federal list of protected species, along with comparable state protections. The current list of AGFC's endangered species list and protections can be found in Arkansas Game and Fish Commission's Code of Regulations.

## Optimal

This habitat type is ideal for this species for at least one stage of its life cycle, but it may persist without it during that stage or otherwise.

## Suitable

This habitat type is near-ideal habitat for this species for at least one stage of its life cycle and it may persist solely within it for either just that stage or indefinitely.

## Associated

This species may utilize this habitat type or a resource within during at least one stage of its life cycle, but it cannot persist solely within it.

## Unsuitable

This species does not utilize or cannot persist solely within this habitat type during any stage of its life cycle.

## Unknown

It is not known if this species utilizes or can persist within this habitat type during any stage of its life cycle.
* Habitats are assumed to be in ideal conditions.

## Species Distribution

To develop updated distribution maps for each SGCN, species occurrence data were derived from several sources, including public agencies, private nonprofit organizations, academic institutions, museum collections, scientific publications, and field surveys. Additional data were obtained from other sources when records were unavailable or limited, such as eBird, Global
Biodiversity Information Facility (GBIF), and iNaturalist. If location data was available for a species, it is displayed at the county-level on the species profiles maps. Taxa Teams also noted the presence or absence of each species in each ecoregion and river basins, for aquatic species only. See Chapter 3 for descriptions of and more information about each ecoregion and river basin.

## Species Profiles

An overview of the information described here has been developed for each SGCN in a species profile (Appendix A). These Species Profiles are meant to serve as a quick reference guide to identify an SGCN's priority tier, conservation status, general distribution within the state, habitat associations, threats, conservation actions, data gaps, and some other basic information. In line with our efforts to streamline this plan, these are a more condensed version of the Species Reports in the previous versions of the AWAP.

## Taxa Summaries

Presented below are brief introductions to each taxa group. Here, taxa teams offer a general overview of their taxa group by providing a summary of their diversity, general distribution, habitat associations, conservation status, main threats, and monitoring strategy within Arkansas.
A list of taxa team members, or individuals who assisted in compiling and reviewing the species information for the plan, is also included here.
As discussed above, standardized threats and conservation actions were adopted for this revision, as well as the development of standardized data gaps. However, standardized monitoring needs are not included in this plan. Alternatively, monitoring needs are addressed more broadly and at the taxa level.
Monitoring strategies, techniques, and requirements vary widely, but can often be similar for particular taxonomic groups or habitats. While taxa specific monitoring strategies and approaches are discussed below, there are some common themes to addressing this element.
Effectively addressing the problems impacting these species and their habitats requires monitoring the status and response of the species over time. to and winter in central and South America, returning to North America in early-mid spring.
These Arkansas breeding birds rely on habitats on their wintering ground in the Southern
Hemisphere that have and continue to undergo habitat alterations and destruction.
Conservation of these Arkansas SGCN must extend to their full life cycle and include the restoration and protection of the winter habitats on which they depend in Central and South America. While there are birding hotspots, there are not biodiversity hotspots for birds in Arkansas given the different habitat preferences and needs of the different bird guilds and species. The degree of biodiversity also depends on the time of year (migration, winter, summer), the quality of the resources in that year (wet, dry) and on scale. Bald Knob NWR during
August is extremely diverse but has little diversity in June compared to the Ozark Mountains in May during migration.
Native wild birds are protected under the federal Migratory Bird Treaty Act and/or by Arkansas Game and Fish Commission's gamebird regulations, the Bald and Golden Eagle Protection Act, or the Endangered Species Act. Three bird species that have been identified as SGCN are also federally listed as Threatened by the U.S. Fish and Wildlife Service; Red-cockaded Woodpecker, Eastern Black Rail and Piping Plover, with no bird SGCN having been designated as Endangered".
In general, the greatest threats to Arkansas birds are related to habitat. Habitat loss, habitat degradation and habitat fragmentation are among the greatest threats faced by Arkansas' avian SGCN. Anthropomorphic threats to birds are significant and include collisions with structures and vehicles, and predation by outdoor domestic cats. Information regarding the specific threats faced by individual bird species, their unique conservation needs, existing data gaps, and habitat associations are documented within their individual species profiles.
Monitoring of bird populations is necessary to identify and evaluate the impacts of threats and targeted habitat conservation strategies to address those threats, to determine both short-and long-term trends in their populations. Additionally, this information may be used to guide and focus research efforts on identifying unknown causes of declines, severity of threats and impacts, and to inform management decisions to help mitigate or reverse those declines. Avian monitoring as it relates to the Arkansas Wildlife Action Plan (AWAP) is one of the Plan's most important components because it enables agencies and organizations to determine the success of restoration and management activities, leads directly to evaluations of the effectiveness of restoration and enables additional more informed habitat management to better meet need the needs of species and species groups dependent on specific habitat conditions.
Monitoring is an integral part of adaptive management used in the AWAP. Monitoring strategies for reptiles are highly diverse and dependent on the habitats utilized by the target species. Drift fences equipped with motion triggered game cameras rather than pitfall traps have become increasingly popular methods for detecting presence/absence of rare species to determine distribution. Trapping with hoop traps for aquatic turtles and with pitfall traps for terrestrial taxa paired with mark recapture techniques is necessary to determine population densities and collect demographic information and to highlight target areas for conservation action. Emerging infectious diseases like Ranaviruses and Snake Fungal Diseases are also a priority for our highest priority turtle species and our SGCN snake species. Information regarding the specific threats faced by individual reptile species, their unique conservation needs, existing data gaps, and habitat associations documented within their individual species profiles.

The class Amphibia is made up of three orders:
Gymnophiona which includes caecilians; Urodela which includes salamanders and newts; and
Anura which includes frogs and toads. Amphibians also have a wide variety of life history strategies. Some species are fully terrestrial direct developers that go directly from egg to juvenile without the classic tadpole or larval stage we typically associate with amphibians. Some species are fully aquatic and paedomorphic meaning they never lose their larval characteristics even as adults. Some species are biphasic and use aquatic habitats to go through several larval developmental stages then using terrestrial habitats after metamorphosis is complete.
Amphibians are the most imperiled vertebrate taxa group on Earth, with 40.7% of species being threatened globally 
(Luedtke et al., 2023)
. This pattern is true for the amphibian species of The major threats facing amphibians are habitat loss, fragmentation, and degradation as well as emerging infectious diseases (e.g., Chytrid fungus, Salamander Chytrid fungus, and Ranavirus).
Semi-aquatic species of amphibians require both terrestrial and aquatic habitats to complete their life cycles. This dependence on multiple habitat types, as well as their inability to disperse during the larval stage, puts amphibians at an increased risk of declines due to habitat fragmentation compared to other more mobile taxa as it can disconnect populations of amphibians from one of these vital habitats. Due to their complexity in body shape, developmental strategy, and habitat use, conservation management for amphibians can be challenging because species employing different combinations of these factors often require species specific monitoring strategies and conservation actions. Monitoring efforts for amphibians can include swabbing and disease monitoring for both variants of Chytrid fungus and Ranaviruses, spring call surveys for frogs to estimate densities of breeding populations, fall drift fence surveys for fall breeding Ambystoma salamanders, dip-net surveys for frog and salamander larvae, visual surveys for Plethodon salamanders and artificial nest box maintenance for endangered Ozark Hellbenders. Information regarding the specific threats faced by individual amphibian species, their unique conservation needs, existing data gaps, and habitat associations are documented within their individual species profiles.
Taxa Team Members: Amanda Bryant (AGFC), Kory Roberts (HerpsofArkansas.com), Don Shepard (University of Arkansas, Fayetteville), Kelly Irwin (AGFC, retired) Taxa Summary: Fish Fish are the most diverse vertebrate group, with over 32,000 described species inhabiting nearly every aquatic environment. This incredible diversity is reflected in their stunning array of body shapes and colors. Within Arkansas, the size of fish ranges dramatically, from the tiny 1.5-inch Least Darter to the impressive 8-foot-long Alligator Gar. Coloration is particularly brilliant in Arkansas from early spring through summer months, coinciding with spawning periods. Fish exhibit a wide spectrum of behaviors and life history characteristics, employing diverse reproductive strategies such as broadcast spawning, nests construction with parental care, and even live birth. Their feeding habits are equally varied, encompassing diets of macroinvertebrates, other fish, herbivorous matter, and filter-feeding. Fish are critical components of many ecosystems providing various ecosystem services, and also hold substantial economic and recreational importance within the state.
Despite freshwater habitats constituting a mere 0.01% of the planet's total water resources, they support almost half of all known fish species. The diverse fish fauna of Arkansas inhabit a wide variety of aquatic environments, from the unique conditions of caves and springs to the clear, flowing waters of upland streams and the expansive reaches of large rivers. Areas of particularly high fish diversity, often referred to as biodiversity hotspots, include the Ozark and Ouachita mountain regions. These areas are home to a significant number of endemic and rare species that are especially vulnerable to alterations in their stream habitats. The lowland regions of the state also support a distinct fish community, characterized by several regionally endemic taxa. It is not uncommon to find 15-25 different fish species at a single sampling location, and in some river systems, over 60 species can be encountered within a one-kilometer stretch, highlighting the rich biodiversity of fish in Arkansas.
Fish populations in Arkansas are confronted with a complex and interacting array of threats impacting their distribution, abundance, and survival. Habitat loss and degradation are significant concerns, stemming from various human activities such as urbanization, agricultural practices, and resource extraction. These activities often lead to physical destruction or alteration of critical fish habitats, including spawning grounds, nurseries, feeding areas, and migration corridors. Alterations to natural flow regimes, resulting from dam construction, water diversions, insufficient road crossings, and changes in land use, also pose substantial threats. The physical fragmentation of aquatic habitats by human-made structures can isolate populations and limit their ability to move and reproduce, reducing the resilience of fish populations to other stressors. Pollution from various sources, including industrial discharge, agricultural runoff, and domestic waste, can degrade water quality and harm fish directly or indirectly through impacts on their food sources. Overexploitation through unsustainable fishing practices can also deplete fish populations, particularly those with limited distributions or slow reproductive rates.
Additionally, the introduction and spread of invasive species can outcompete native fish for resources, prey upon them, or introduce diseases, further jeopardizing their survival. These threats often act synergistically, creating cumulative impacts that can be particularly challenging to address. Information regarding the specific threats faced by individual fish species, their unique conservation needs, and existing data gaps are documented within their individual species profiles.
The approach to monitoring fish populations in Arkansas is not uniform and varies depending on the specific species, their distribution, and the conservation concerns associated with them.
Factors such as the species' size, behavior, habitat use, and life cycle stage influence the choice of gear and sampling design. Species identified as threatened, endangered, or of conservation concern often require more frequent and detailed monitoring to assess declines, identify Arkansas is home to 60 species of crayfish including 43 SGCN, 26 regional endemics, 2 federally endangered cave crayfish, and 2 species of shrimp in the family Palaemonidae. These 60 crayfish species exhibit a large range of life-history strategies and are found in a wide variety of habitats throughout Arkansas. All crayfish need water to survive, and all crayfish burrow. Crayfish are often further divided into primary, secondary, and tertiary burrowers based on their morphology, habitat use, and burrowing ability. Primary burrowers are powerful burrowers and are sometimes found in seemingly dry places, like Arkansas's Boston Mountains, where the Boston Mountains Burrowing Crayfish (Cambarus causeyi) build deep complex burrows to find water or utilize natural springs in the area to survive. Secondary burrowers, like Arkansas' endemic Bayou Bodcau Crayfish (Bouchardina robisoni), build simple burrows, often in the bottom of swamps or on river banks. Tertiary burrowers remain in rivers and streams throughout their life-cycle and burrow under rocks for shelter. This large variation in life-history strategies and habitat-use means the sampling methods, threats, and conservation actions needed to protect these species are just as varied.
Crayfish biodiversity hotspots include the Ozark and Ouachita Mountain regions that are both distinct from each other and diverse especially in regards to tertiary burrowers. The Coastal Plain has a high diversity of primary and secondary burrowers, many of which are regional endemics.
Despite the high level of diversity in these regions, it is uncommon to find more than 5 different crayfish species at a single sampling location; instead crayfish are diverse across the landscape since they occur almost anywhere there is consistent water, even underground.
Crayfish in Arkansas face a variety of complex threats that affect their ability to persist in a variety of habitats. Invasive species are a major concern, as they can outcompete native crayfish for resources, introduce disease, or prey directly on them, and some of our highest priority species are currently being invaded by other crayfish species. Pollution can degrade water quality above and below ground impacting crayfish both directly or by harming their food sources or habitat. Habitat fragmentation caused by dams, roads, and other barriers can isolate populations and prevent them from moving freely or reaching important breeding or feeding areas. This is especially true for burrowing crayfish populations which can be compressed into roadside ditches by urbanization then fragmented away from other populations. Habitat loss and degradation, often driven by urbanization, agriculture, and resource extraction, continue to be widespread and damaging, often impacting key habitats like springs and wetlands. These stressors frequently interact, creating compounding effects that can be difficult to address with a single management action. Information regarding the specific threats faced by individual crayfish species, their unique conservation needs, existing data gaps, and habitat associations are documented within their individual species profiles.
Monitoring approaches must be unique for primary, secondary, and tertiary burrowers, due to differing habitat use. Monitoring for primary burrowers consists of burrow counts along a transect to estimate the number of primary burrowers present, followed by excavation of burrows to determine the proportion of each species present. Monitoring of secondary burrowers is often conducted in wetlands where a catch per unit effort is calculated using seining or dip nets. Monitoring tertiary burrowers in wadable streams is conducted at monitoring sites using a box seine to estimate crayfish density at each site. In clear large rivers divers hand collect crayfish and a catch per unit effort is calculated.
Taxa Team Members: Maxwell Hartman (AGFC), Dustin Lynch (ANHC), Katie Morris (ANHC) Taxa Summary: Mussels
Freshwater mussels, also known as freshwater clams, naiads, and unionids, are closely related to marine clams. Scientifically speaking, a freshwater mussel is a bivalved mollusk that lives in freshwater habitats. Their anatomy includes two shells connected by a hinge-like ligament.
Freshwater mussels differ from marine clams in their unique life-cycle requirements. They are obligate parasites of fishes, meaning they require a host fish to complete their life cycle. Adult mussels exhibit a significant range in size, from as small as a thumbnail to as large as a pie plate.
Their diverse shapes and colors are evident in species with names like Purple Wartyback, Arkansas Fatmucket, and Purple Lilliput. Freshwater mussels are also known for their longevity, being the longest-lived invertebrates. While some have been recorded to live for over a century, a typical lifespan ranges from 10 to 30 years, varying by species. Similar to tree rings, the growth lines visible on their shells often correspond to years in their life. Partially burrowing into the bottoms of streambeds, they are often only noticeable by their two small siphons, which they use to filter water. These remarkable creatures function as natural water filters, with the ability to process up to eight gallons of water daily through their internal systems. Given their capacity to filter out contaminants and suspended sediments, their contribution to maintaining clean water cannot be overstated.
The United States boasts an extraordinary diversity of freshwater mussels, holding approximately 300 of the world's estimated 1,000 species. This remarkable figure represents about one-third of the global mussel fauna. When compared to other regions, such as Africa with 96 species, China with 60, and Europe with only 12, the richness of mussel life in the U.S. is truly significant, with the majority of this diversity concentrated in the southeastern United States. However, this incredible biodiversity is facing a serious threat. It is estimated that over 70% of the mussel species in the U.S. are in peril, making them the most imperiled group of animals in the nation.
Tragically, over seven percent of all mussel species have already gone extinct, and an additional 50% are now under federal or state protection. The situation in Arkansas mirrors this trend, with 14 of our currently 77 recognized mussel species being federally protected (with two additional species proposed for listing) and 40 (52%) of them being designated as Species of Greatest Conservation Need (SGCN). Furthermore, 2 of these species are only found in Arkansas, along with another 17 species that are endemic to the region.
The SGCN species are further categorized into tiers based on the severity of the threats they face. The Highest Tier includes 19 species (47%), indicating the most urgent conservation concerns. The High Tier includes only six species while the Moderate Tier includes 15 species (37%). While there has been a precipitous decline in freshwater mussels in Arkansas there is only one mussel species that is officially recognized as extinct, the Turgid Blossom, which was last seen in 1972.
These mussels inhabit various freshwater environments, with the highest concentrations found in streams and rivers. Within these flowing water habitats mussels are typically found in the vicinity of riffles and shoals but can also occur in other areas of the river depending on the specific species. Arkansas's rivers that harbor diverse mussel communities are the Ouachita, Saline, Spring, and White rivers.
Freshwater mussels in Arkansas face numerous threats, including: habitat loss due to damming rivers; altered stream habitats from channelization, dredging, and gravel mining; erosion from unpaved roads, logging, and industrial agriculture leads to increased sediment in waterways, smothering mussels and filling their habitats; pollution from agricultural and industrial runoff and wastewater treatment plants, which can directly harm mussels and degrade water quality; and invasive species like Asiatic clams and zebra mussels that compete with native mussels for food and can overwhelm their habitats. Additionally, declining host fish populations that mussels rely on to complete their complex life cycle can disrupt mussel recruitment and lead to population declines. Information regarding the specific threats faced by individual mussel species, their unique conservation needs, existing data gaps, and habitat associations are documented within their individual species profiles.
Freshwater mussel monitoring in Arkansas involves assessing the health and distribution of mussel populations to understand their role in river ecosystems and identify conservation needs.
Monitoring is accomplished through two different survey methodologies (qualitative vs quantitative) that provide similar but variable information on the condition of mussel populations. Qualitative surveys provide basic information on mussels and are useful in determining presence, a coarse measure of abundance, species composition, and habitat suitability while providing limited insight into the health of a species or mussel populations.
Quantitative sampling is more robust in determining the health of mussel populations by providing measurable estimates of density, recent recruitment and population dynamics, and measures of body condition leading to a more informative assessment. While quantitative sampling is extremely labor intensive it is the most informative sampling design when conducting sampling associated with long-term monitoring of a species or locality, determining changes in habitat and how mussels are responding to those changes, and determining the response of mussels to conservation actions and helping determine the effectiveness of conservation actions. These sampling methodologies will be used for periodic monitoring of SGCN and their habitats, for monitoring the effectiveness of conservation actions, and for adapting conservation actions as appropriate to respond to new information or changing conditions. Monitoring of freshwater mussels is crucial because these bivalves are extremely sensitive to habitat and environmental changes, serving as indicators of water quality and aquatic habitat health.
Taxa Team Members: Kendall Moles (AGFC), Bill Posey (AGFC, retired), Chris Davidson (USFWS), John Harris (Arkansas State University) Taxa Summary: Insects and Other Invertebrates
Insects and other invertebrates (e.g., Arachnids, Myriapods, Isopods, Amphipods, Terrestrial Gastropods, etc.) constitute the vast majority of animal biodiversity, serving as fundamental components of nearly all terrestrial and aquatic ecosystems. Their ecological contributions are invaluable, upholding the function, health, and resiliency of our ecosystems. This includes performing critical ecosystem services, such as pollination, decomposition, nutrient cycling, soil aeration, water filtration, and pest control, as well as serving as important food sources for various other taxa. Insects and other invertebrates inhabit every habitat type in Arkansas, from caves and springs to prairies and woodlands to rivers and streams.
Compared to other taxa groups, insects and other invertebrates, as a whole, are vastly understudied. This is in part due to the sheer number and diversity of species and their life histories, size, and often elusiveness. This makes trying to assess the status of these species and developing conservation actions, if needed, very difficult. However, this does not mean that we should forego trying to conserve, study, and protect these species. To accomplish this task, a strategic approach is required. With the high number of species in this group that are likely atrisk, the SGCN list could very quickly become overwhelmed. Our approach to this issue was to identify species where sufficient data was available to determine their status or representative species from various groups that would serve as umbrella species. Meaning the conservation of those species would also benefit similar species or others that utilize the same habitats. A couple of focus groups were pollinators and cave or karst-dependent species.
Using this strategy, we identified 139 SGCN in this taxa group, occupying 26 orders and 53 families. Of these species, 46 of them are endemic to Arkansas, and 51 of them karst-dependent species, illustrating the diversity and importance of cave and karst systems in the state. Sixty-two of the SGCN are in the Highest priority tier, 37 in the High tier, 23 in the Moderate tier, and 17 of them are classified as Data Deficient.
Insects and other invertebrates are plagued by numerous and often compounding threats. This includes habitat loss and degradation, pesticides, pollution, and climate change. Different species may be more susceptible to some threats than others, but the combination of some of these threats can be multifaceted and detrimental to populations or species. Subsequently, it important to monitor the impact and severity of those threats on species.
Effective monitoring is crucial for understanding population trends, evaluating habitat health, and determining the success of conservation efforts and management actions. For both aquatic and terrestrial invertebrates, monitoring strategies should be integrated and adaptive, drawing from existing programs or efforts to maximize resources and efficiency. Aquatic invertebrates are exceptional indicators of ecosystem health, providing insights into water quality conditions, and occurrence and abundance data can be obtained from water quality sampling and monitoring efforts. For terrestrial invertebrates, there are several utilized trapping or surveying methods, such as blacklight traps, pitfall traps, and sweep-netting. These are mostly semiselective trapping methods, and data can be collected for various species, besides the targeted species, when utilizing these trapping methods. Community science efforts and tools, like iNaturalist, have also become a valuable method for monitoring species, by improving survey capabilities and generating widespread and publicly available data. This is especially true for pollinators. Additionally, two current SWG projects are working to determine occurrence and distribution of native bees and firefly species in Arkansas.
We will utilize all available sources to compile species and population data and information to help inform species statuses, needed conservation actions, and the success of conservation and habitat management efforts. 

## The Ecoregions of Arkansas

Ecoregions denote areas of general similarity in ecosystems and in the type, quality, and quantity of environmental resources. They are designed to serve as a spatial framework for the research, assessment, management and monitoring of ecosystems and ecosystem components.
Ecoregions are general purpose regions that are critical for structuring and implementing ecosystem management strategies across federal agencies, state agencies, and nongovernment organizations that are responsible for different types of resources in the same geographical areas. Upland soils are mostly Ultisols that developed under oak-hickory and oak-hickory-pine forests.
Today, forests are still widespread; northern red oak, southern red oak, white oak and hickories usually dominate the uplands, but shortleaf pine grows on drier, south-and west-facing slopes underlain by sandstone. Both precipitation and forest density decrease toward the west, where oak-pine woodland or savanna become common. This region is more rugged than the Ozark Highlands to the north and prairies were historically smaller and relatively rare, due to smaller natural fire compartments and fewer flat areas. Savannas were likely common on ridgetops and south and western aspect benches, shaped by droughty soil as well as fire.
Today, pastureland or hayland occur on nearly level ridgetops, benches and valley floors.
Population density is low except for the Northwest Arkansas area. The primary land uses are recreation, logging and livestock farming. Some areas have been converted to industrial loblolly pine plantation, though this land use is restricted by landscape conditions.
In the Boston Mountains, water quality in streams is generally exceptional; biochemical, nutrient and mineral water quality parameter concentrations all tend to be very low. During low flows, streams in both the Boston Mountains and the Ozark Highlands usually run clear but, during high flow conditions, turbidity tends to be greater in the Boston Mountains. Summer flow in many small streams is limited or nonexistent but isolated, enduring pools may occur.
Water quality in streams reflects geology, soils and land use and is typically exceptional; mineral, nutrient and solid concentrations as well as turbidity all tend to be very low. During the summer, many streams do not flow.

## Arkansas Valley

The Prairies were also common and widespread where large natural fire compartments exist on plateau tops and valley floors, interspersed with savannas on the slopes. These features were even more prominent in the rainshadow of the Fourche Mountains, displaying lower annual rainfall amounts than would be predicted for the area. Today, many of these prairies have been converted to pasture and hay meadow, some of which still contain remnant prairie vegetation and are easily restorable. Valley prairies were preferentially selected for settlements and today many former prairies are now urban centers. Between the levees that parallel the Mississippi River is a corridor known as the "batture lands".
Batture lands are hydrologically linked to the Mississippi River, flood-prone and contain remnant habitat for "big river" species (e.g., pallid sturgeon) as well as river-front plant communities.
Earthquakes in the early nineteenth century offset river courses. Small to medium size earthquakes still occur frequently; their shocks are magnified by the alluvial plain's unconsolidated deposits, creating regional land management issues. "Sand blows" and "sunk lands" occur and have been attributed to the New Madrid earthquakes of 1811-12 (approximately magnitude 8).
Within the meander belt of the Mississippi River and its major tributaries, point bars, natural Narrow floodplains had bottomland hardwood forests.
Cropland has now largely replaced the native vegetation. In the process, some prairie species have been extirpated from the ecoregion (e.g., greater prairie chicken); others have been sharply
reduced in population and restricted to a few prairie remnants. Distinctively, rice is the main crop; soybeans, cotton, corn and wheat are also grown. Aquaculture farms are also common.
This region provides important habitat for fish and wildlife and includes the largest continuous system of wetlands in North America. It is also a major bird migration corridor used in fall and spring migrations. Rice fields provide habitat and forage for large numbers and many species of waterfowl and provide ample opportunities for duck and goose hunting.
Streams and rivers have very low gradients and fine-grained substrates. Many reaches have illdefined stream channels. Agricultural runoff containing fertilizers, herbicides, pesticides and livestock waste have degraded surficial water quality.
Concentrations of total suspended solids, total dissolved solids, total phosphorus, ammonia nitrogen, sulfates, turbidity, biological oxygen demand, chlorophyll a and fecal coliform are high in the rivers, streams and ditches; they are often much greater than elsewhere in Arkansas, increase with increasing watershed size and are greatest during the spring, high-flow season.
Man-made flood control levees typically flank the Mississippi River and, in effect, separate the river and its adjoining habitat from the remainder of its natural hydrologic system; in so doing, they interfere with sediment transfer and have reduced available habitat for many species. 

## Ouachita River Basin

The Ouachita River originates at the bases of Rich and Black Fork mountains in the Ouachita Mountains of Arkansas and flows 362 miles southeast from the mountains onto the Coastal Plain as it winds its way south toward its eventual junction with the Red River at Alexandria, Louisiana.
Within Arkansas, the Ouachita River drains 11,548 square miles.
The Ouachita River is the second-largest drainage in the state that has most of its headwaters, the entire main stem transitional zone between upland and lowland stream, and most of the lowland portion of the main stem within Arkansas.

## Red River Basin

The 

## Riparian and Floodplains

## Riparian

Riparian refers to an area that is directly adjacent to a flowing body of water such as a stream or river. Riparian areas are the transition zone between the body of water and the surrounding landscape. They are typically forested but sometimes consist of shrub cover. These areas are important for holding soil in place during flood events and for shading streams.
Ozarks and Ouachitas: Ozark-Ouachita riparian habitats occur along streams and small rivers and often contain cobble bars and steep banks. It is traditionally higher gradient than larger floodplains and experiences periodic, strong flooding. Canopy cover can vary within examples of this system, but typical tree species include sweetgum, sycamore, maples, and oaks. The herbaceous layer can vary from sparse to dense. Likewise, the shrub layer can vary considerably, but typical species may include spicebush, smooth alder, and witch hazel. Small seeps and fens can often be found, especially at the headwaters of streams. These areas are typically dominated by species of sedges, ferns, and other herbaceous species. Flooding and scouring strongly influence this system and prevent the floodplain development found on larger rivers.
West Gulf Coastal Plain: In the West Gulf Coastal Plain, riparian habitats occur along small rivers and creeks. These areas are characterized by bottomland hardwood tree species with mesic hardwood species in less flood prone areas. Flooding occurs annually but not typically during the growing season.
Mississippi Alluvial Valley: In the Mississippi Alluvial Valley, riparian systems are composed of riverfront associations, generally temporarily (but rarely seasonally) flooded on point bars and natural levees adjacent to the river that formed them, with flooding more frequent than every 5 years, by flowing water directly from the stream. They occur along the lower Mississippi River and its tributaries. Flooding is of lower duration than on adjacent backswamps, where water is impounded behind riverfront natural levees. Flooding is of longer duration than on high bottomlands that are typically temporarily flooded. Soils are typically sandier than those of low bottomlands. Giant cane (Arundinaria gigantea) is a common understory in these forests on natural levees and higher point bars, and may become dominant after thinning or removal of overstory.

## Floodplains

Floodplains are land surfaces adjacent to rivers or streams that are formed in part by the deposition of sediment when the water overflows the banks of the channel. In general, floodplains flanking high gradient rivers, and rivers with small drainage areas, have more frequent and "flashier" periods of inundation, whereas floodplains along very low gradient rivers with large drainage areas have fewer peak events with a smoother flood pulse 
(Mitsch and Gosselink, 2000)
. Floodplains are dynamic systems that support biodiversity and control sediment movement.
Ozarks and Ouachitas: Floodplains in the Ozarks and Ouachitas occur along larger upland rivers (ex. Buffalo River). Many examples of this system will contain well-drained levees, terraces and stabilized bars, and some will include herbaceous sloughs and shrub wetlands resulting, in part, from beaver activity. A variety of soil types may be found within the floodplain from very well drained, gravelly substrates to very dense clays. This variety of substrates in combination with different flooding regimes creates a mix of vegetation. Most areas are inundated at some point each spring; microtopography determines how long the various habitats are inundated. Although vegetation is variable in this broadly defined system, examples may include sugar maple, sycamore, sweetgum, river birch, and oaks. Understory species include shrubs, such as buttonbush and rivercane, and sedges. Flooding occurs annually and the habitat may be altered by occasional severe floods. Impoundments and conversion to agriculture can also impact these floodplains.
West Gulf Coastal Plain: In the West Gulf Coastal Plain, floodplains occur along larger rivers such as the Saline and Ouachita. Several distinct plant communities are recognized within this system that may be related to the array of different geomorphic features present within the floodplain.
Some of the major geomorphic features associated with different community types include natural levees, point bars, meander scrolls, oxbows and sloughs. However, in many cases these features are too small to be mapped or managed individually, and therefore contribute to an overall matrix of the habitat. Vegetation generally includes forests dominated by bottomland hardwood species and other trees tolerant of flooding and distributed according to these microsite variations.

Taxa teams then updated descriptions of these habitat types and developed management and restoration recommendations and Desired Future Conditions for the manageable natural habitat types. This information was then used to develop new habitat profiles.Since the last revision, Arkansas has established 19 Conservation Opportunity Areas (COA) to identify priority areas for conservation, restoration, and protection efforts. AGFC has partnered with The Nature Conservancy (TNC) to develop specific Conservation Plans for each COA. A discussion of how the COAs were developed (Chapter 4), and a brief overview of the conservation plans has been included in this revision (Appendix C).Conservation Partner InvolvementConservation partners were informed of the planned changes to the Plan at the 2024 AWAP Symposium. Their input was requested for what changes they would like to see made or incorporated into the revised Plan to improve its usefulness to their organizations. This was mainly through participation in taxa and habitat team efforts to review and update species conservation status ranks, help develop the new SGCN list, identify threats, conservation actions, data gaps, habitat associations, and other species information, and the review and update of habitat types, descriptions, and management and restoration recommendations.An email notification was sent to all partners on August 4, 2025, with a link to the document, a survey, and an email address for additional comments, requesting their review and feedback on the draft plan. A thirty-day review period was given. All feedback will be reviewed and considered for incorporation into the final Plan. Public Involvement Public participation is an important component of any conservation plan. Public input was requested for the Plan following a similar procedure for the conservation partners. [FIGURE: Figure 3.1. Level III ecoregions in Arkansas (adapted from Woods et al. 2004).]

[FIGURE: Arkansas Valley is a synclinal and alluvial valley lying between the Ozark Highlands and the Ouachita Mountains. The Arkansas Valley is, characteristically, diverse and transitional. It generally coincides with the Arkoma Basin, an oil and gas province, that developed as sand and mud were deposited in a depression north of the rising Ouachita Mountains during the Mississippian and Pennsylvanian eras. The Arkansas Valley contains plains, hills, floodplains, terraces and scattered mountains. It is largely underlain by interbedded Pennsylvanian sandstone, shale and siltstone. Prior to the 19th century, uplands were dominated by a mix of forest, woodland, savanna and prairie whereas floodplains and lower terraces were covered by bottomland deciduous forest. More rugged areas were covered in open woodlands codominated by upland oaks, hickory and shortleaf pine. Magazine Mountain, the highest point in Arkansas at 2,753 feet and similar mountains including Petit Jean and Nebo, are distinguished by diverse habitats. Their flat tops are covered with dry, stunted woodlands. Wetter sites also occur on the mountain tops and may contain beech-maple forests. Today, historically open woodlands have largely suffered from mesophication and fire protection, though some have been converted to pasture or loblolly pine production.]

[FIGURE: Red River flows for approximately 135 miles (217 km) through the extreme southwestern portion of Arkansas draining 4,433 sq. miles of Arkansas before it continues south into Louisiana to join the Atchafalaya River. The Red River formerly flowed directly into the Mississippi River in Louisiana, but the flood of 1927 and the subsequent construction of levees diverted its flow southward into the Atchafalaya River (Douglas 1974). The Arkansas portion of the Red River has not been impounded, but the lower portion of the Red River downstream from US Route 71 has been modified by man-made levees, revetted banks, and wing dikes. Upstream from U.S. 71 to the Oklahoma state line, there are few channel modification structures (Buchanan et al. 2003). The most important tributaries of the Red River system in Arkansas are the Little River, McKinney chert in the Springfield Plateau are occupied by short-leaf pine-hardwood forests and woodlands, whereas areas underlain by limestone (calcareous) rock are occupied by dry to mesic hardwood forests and woodlands. The Salem Plateau is primarily underlain by dolomite with clay residuum and is covered with open oak woodlands and dry-mesic oak forests. In the Ouachitas, forests and woodlands occur as pine and pine-hardwood forests on southfacing slopes and dry-mesic hardwood forests on north-facing slopes. The Pine-bluestem forest is unique to the Ouachitas and consists of open canopy shortleaf pine and a very grassy understory. In the highest elevations of the Ouachitas, such as Mount Magazine, there are montane oak forests. These small, isolated systems can occur as forests or open woodlands dominated by often stunted white oak or post oak. Throughout the Ozarks and Ouachitas, forested seeps may occur along the bottoms of slopes where rock fractures or sandy soils allow water to seep out of the mountainside. The canopy of seeps can be closed to open and the subcanopy is often well developed. West Gulf Coastal Plain: In the West Gulf Coastal Plain, forests and woodlands can occur as mixed pine hardwoods in wet or dry conditions. Sandhill oak and shortleaf pine forest and woodlands occur on uplands underlain with sandy soils and are characterized by patches of bare soil and sparse vegetation. Bluejack oak and Arkansas oak are the dominant tree species in sandhill forests along with shortleaf pine. Pine-hardwood flatwood forests and woodlands typically occur outside of the floodplain on higher terraces. Loblolly pine, cherrybark oak, and willow oak are dominant trees over a grassy understory.Crowley's Ridge: Forests on the southern portion of Crowley's Ridge are confined to a series of narrow ridges and are unique in the surrounding agricultural landscape. These ridges and slopes have plant species that are absent from other parts of the Mississippi Alluvial Plain, such as tuliptree, climbing magnolia, and Virginia pennywort. Other common trees are American beech and white oak. In the northern portion of Crowley's Ridge, the soils are sandier and the forests include some of the only naturally occurring shortleaf pine in eastern Arkansas. Other trees are white oak, post oak, and southern red oak. These systems are fire adapted and current fire suppression has resulted in altered species composition.Mississippi Alluvial Valley: Forests in the Mississippi Alluvial Valley can be generally characterized as high and low bottomland forest, dune ponds, or flatwoods. High bottomlands flood temporarily with a frequency of less than every 5 years. This system has been cleared more frequently given the drier conditions. Dominant tree species are cherrybark oak and swamp chestnut oak. Low bottomlands are usually seasonally flooded in backswamps, with flooding more frequent than every 5 years, usually more frequently than every two years, generally by still water that may be impounded behind natural levees. Overcup oak is the dominant tree species. Dune ponds are distinctive wetlands called "sand ponds" that occur in depressions and have silty bottoms.Flatwoods include forests, woodlands, and prairies that have fragipans, poor internal drainage and are flat with poor runoff, leading to wet conditions in winter and spring. The seasonally wet and dry soil moistures result in a variety of community types with dominant tree species ranging from post oak (drier) to overcup oak (wetter). Much of this habitat has been cleared for plantation or agricultural use and remaining stands are fragmented and subject to invasive species infestations.]

Arkansas is home to 247 of the ~1,075 species of fish found in North America. This total includes 27 introduced species and 220 native species, classified across 81 genera, 28 families, and 19 orders. * The state of Arkansas occupies a unique position in the botanical landscape of North America. It is a biological meeting ground where plant assemblages from the Great Plains to the west, the Mississippi River floodplain to the east, the Gulf Coastal Plain to the south, and the temperate deciduous forests to the north converge. This geographic confluence, layered upon a complex geological and climatic history, has endowed the state with a rich and diverse native flora.From bottomland hardwood forests and swamps in the Mississippi Alluvial Valley to Ozark Mountain caves, forests, glades, and streams. This diversity supports an array of wildlife and countless opportunities for outdoor recreation. Arkansas's ecological diversity is strongly related to regional physiography, geology, soil, climate and land use. In Arkansas, there are seven level III ecoregions(Woods et al. 2004). For the purposes of this plan, we have lumped the Boston Mountain ecoregion into the Ozark Mountain ecoregion (Figure3.1).*
