* The floodplain of the Arkansas River and its major tributaries are characteristically veneered with Holocene alluvium and include natural levees, meander scars, oxbow lakes, point bars, swales and backswamps. It is lithologically and physiographically distinct from the surrounding uplands of the Arkansas Valley. Mollisols, Entisols, Alfisols and Inceptisols are common; the soil mosaic sharply contrasts with nearby, higher elevation ecoregions where Ultisols developed under upland oaks, hickory and pine. Potential natural vegetation is southern floodplain forest.Water quality is generally good and influenced more by land use activities than by soils or geology; average stream gradients and dissolved oxygen levels are lower in the Arkansas Valley than in the Ouachita Mountains or Ozark Highlands, whereas turbidity, total suspended solids, total organic carbon, total phosphorus and biochemical oxygen demand values are typically higher. The Arkansas River is continuously turbid. Summer flow in smaller streams is typically limited or nonexistent. made up of east-west running ridges, hills and valleys formed by the erosion of folded and faulted Paleozoic sandstone, shale and chert, known locally as novaculite. They are a continuation of the Appalachians, formed during the late Paleozoic Era when an ocean closed and continents collided, causing marine sediments to be folded, faulted and thrust northward.Differences in moisture and temperature between north-and south-facing slopes significantly influence native plant communities; they are products of the prevailing topographic trend.Potential natural vegetation is oak-hickory-pine forest; it contrasts with the oak-hickory forest that dominates the Ozarks and the northern part of the Boston Mountains. Today, loblolly pine and shortleaf pine grow in a distinctive mix of thermic Ultisols and Inceptisols.Today, pine plantations are widespread. Intact mixed hardwood-shortleaf pine forests are also very common, though most have experienced densification from a lack of fire. Valley floors are typically converted to pasture and hay meadow, with poultry production very common.Regional water quality is influenced by lithology, soil composition and land use activities. In most reaches, water quality is exceptional; typically, total phosphorus, turbidity, total suspended solids and biological oxygen demand values are lower whereas dissolved oxygen levels are higher than in Gulf Coastal Plain, Arkansas Valley and Mississippi Alluvial Plain ecoregions. Water hardness varies by sub-ecoregion. Stream substrates are made up of gravel, cobbles, boulders, or bedrock; they contrast with the fine-grained substrates of lower gradient streams in the West Gulf Coastal Plain and Mississippi Alluvial Plain.Perennial springs and seeps are common and support diverse vegetation. Constricted valleys between ridges have waterfalls and rapids. Nutrient, mineral and biochemical water quality parameter concentrations are low in the surface waters of Fourche Mountains but turbidity can be higher than in other mountainous parts of the Ouachitas.The West Gulf Coastal Plains ecoregion includes the Red River bottomlands and blackland prairie communities. The nearly flat Red River Bottomlands ecoregion is veneered with Holocene alluvium and has been widely cleared and drained for agriculture. It contains floodplains, low terraces, oxbow lakes, meander scars, backswamps, natural levees and the meandering Red much higher than in the Saline or Ouachita rivers due to land cover, land use and upstream lithology differences.*

* levees, swales and abandoned channels marked by meander scars and oxbow lakes are common and characteristic. Soils on natural levees are relatively coarse-textured, well-drained and higher than those on levee back slopes and point bars; they grade to very heavy, poorly-drained clays in abandoned channels and swales. Potential natural vegetation is largely southern floodplain forest and is unlike the oak-hickory and oak-hickory-pine forests that dominate uplands to the west; loblolly pine, so common in the West Gulf Coastal Plain, is not native to most forests in the Arkansas portion of the Mississippi Alluvial Valley. Some areas within these meander belts feature a flat to irregular alluvial plain composed of sandy to gravelly glacial outwash overlain by alluvium, resulting in unique plant communities more similar to droughty soils in the Ouachitas than to surrounding areas. Many interdunal depressions called "sandponds" occur and are either in contact with the water table or have a perched aquifer. Elevations are higher than adjacent lands and consequently, uplands are rarely if ever flooded.Outside of the meander belts are backswamps, made up of low-lying overflow areas on floodplains and includes poorly-drained flats and swales. Water often collects in its marshes, swamps, oxbow lakes, ponds and low gradient streams.Prior to the 19th century, flatter areas with slowly to very slowly permeable soils (often containing fragipans) supported Arkansas' largest prairie. They were generally bounded by open woodland or savanna. In all, about 400,000 acres of prairie grasses and forbs occurred in the Grand Prairie and were a sharp contrast to the bottomland forests that once dominated other parts of the Mississippi Alluvial Plain. Low hills were covered by upland deciduous forest containing white oak, black oak and southern red oak. Drier ridges were dominated by post oak.*

* Regulation of White River flow, in combination with the downcutting of the Mississippi River for navigation (and related wing levees and cutoffs), have altered flood regimes on the lower White River, thereby increasing stream bank instability and bottomland forest mortality in the western lowlands holocene meander belts. Most streams and rivers here are fed by the Ozark Highlands and Boston Mountains; sediment load is generally less than in the Mississippi River.Drainage canals and ditches are common. This artificial drainage, together with the sandy veneer of natural levee deposits, help explain why the Arkansas and Ouachita River backswamps are more easily and widely farmed than the Northern Backswamps.River Basins of Arkansas 11 River Basin descriptions are borrowed with permission from Robison and Buchanan 2020.Although all river drainages in Arkansas are part of the large Mississippi River basin, the state can be subdivided into six main river basins for a better explanation and understanding of aquatic species distributional patterns: White River, Arkansas River, Ouachita River, Red River, St. Francis River, and Mississippi River (Figure3.2).The White River drains 27,765 square miles of northern Arkansas and southern Missouri. It is the largest river drainage in Arkansas, draining 17,143 sq. mi. within the state. The large Black River drainage empties into the White River system near Newport, Arkansas, and forms the northeastern part of the White River system. It is the largest stream drainage in which the main stem has its headwaters, transition zone, lowland portion, and mouth within Arkansas (38% of the White River drainage is in Missouri).The White River is quite intriguing since it appears to be a very old river (i.e., has persisted in its present form for a long span of geological time) and consists of three very different ecological regions.Black (1940)  first commented that the White River was rather abruptly divided into three regions: lower, middle, and upper. The large lower part of the White River below Batesville and Black Rock is mostly a lowland river with a slightly silty, large river appearance.The floodplain meanders, and oxbow lakes that characterize a large river are absent upstream of Batesville on the White River proper and slightly above Black Rock, Arkansas, on the Black River. The White River above Batesville is a clear, swift stream of medium size at about Cotter, Arkansas, but most of the mainstem flow today at this locality is regulated by tailwater releases from large dams. The Buffalo River is the major tributary entering the middle section of the White River. This section also makes up an important part of the Arkansas trout fishery.Arkansas River BasinThe Arkansas River bisects Arkansas as it flows from a general northwest to southeast direction for 310 miles through the center of the state, draining 11,100 sq. mi. before it empties into the Mississippi River near Arkansas Post. Tributaries from both the southern Ozark (Mulberry River, Illinois Bayou, Big Piney Creek) and northern Ouachita Mountains (Poteau River, Petit Jean River, Fourche LaFave River, Maumelle River) dilute the turbidity and salinity carried by the Arkansas River as it enters Arkansas from Oklahoma. Farther downstream a few lowland tributaries such as Bayou Meto add to the flow. Its intermediate character in terms of geographic position and fish fauna, makes the Arkansas River difficult to classify. Fenneman (1938) considered it physiographically a separate portion of the Ozark Upland physiographic province, but eastern, western, and southern boundaries of the Arkansas River Valley are not pronounced (Black 1940). The Poteau River is a major tributary of the Arkansas River and penetrates well into the Ouachita Mountains almost to the town of Eagleton, Arkansas, at the foot of Rich Mountain. The lower Poteau River possesses many of the characteristics of a lowland stream where it meets the Arkansas River at Fort Smith, and most of its tributaries are relatively warm (Black 1940).*

## Summary of Changes

The Arkansas Wildlife Action Plan has undergone an in-depth review and redesign to streamline it and enhance its utility, also incorporating new standardized components to improve consistency, clarity, efficiency, and promote landscape-level conservation efforts.
Key highlights of the revised plan include:
• Updated list of 520 Species of Greatest Conservation Need (SGCN), which, for the first time, now includes plants (Chapter 2, Table 
2
.2).
• Incorporated standardized threats, conservation actions, and data gaps that have been identified for each SGCN, providing a clearer roadmap for conservation efforts. (Chapter 2)
• Adopted a new system of Priority Tiers (Highest, High, Moderate, Data Deficient), replacing the previous calculated Priority Scores. This new system aligns with a regional standardization effort for identifying and prioritizing SGCN (Chapter 2).
• Updated habitat types and associations for each SGCN (Chapter 3; Chapter 2).
• Streamlined species profiles for each SGCN, offering specific information on distribution, habitat associations, threats, conservation actions, and data gaps (Appendix A).
• Introduction of habitat guilds (e.g., Forest and Woodlands, Prairies and Savannas, Rivers and Streams, etc.) to simplify discussions about habitat associations (Chapter 3).
• Incorporating 19 recently established Conservation Opportunity Areas (COAs), each with a developed Conservation Plan, which are summarized within the Plan (Chapter 4;Appendix C).
• Completed Ecological Mapping System (EMS) project that produced higher-resolution maps, aiding in conservation and restoration initiatives (Chapter 3).
• Updated and improved habitat profiles that now include detailed Desired Future Conditions (DFCs) and management and restoration recommendations (Appendix B).
• Addition of new regional chapter that discusses how Arkansas's conservation efforts align with and contribute to broader, cross-border landscape-level efforts. This chapter also introduces Regional Species of Greatest Conservation Need (R-SGCN) (Chapter 5).

## Standardized Threats, Conservation Actions, and Data Gaps

In order to further align with national standardization efforts and to more clearly communicate identified threats and needs, we have adopted recommended standardized threats and conservation actions 
(CMP 2016)
 and have developed standardized data gaps, which are foundationally modeled after Texas Parks and Wildlife Department's draft, proposed approach to "strategically prioritize critical conservation management actions while focusing research on removing barriers to conservation management" (A Proposed Framework for Prioritizing SGCN Conservation Research Necessary for Management Actions, 2022). Conservation actions are those that either directly or indirectly aid in the management, restoration, enhancement, or protection of SGCN populations or their habitats. Data gaps are defined as basic research or survey needs that are vital to the development of conservation actions, by removing barriers and providing critical information that is needed to make informed management decisions. Examples include identifying the main threats impacting a species, aspects of their life history, their distribution and abundance, or specific habitat requirements.
The recommended standardized threats and conservation actions are classified into two levels.
When identifying the specific threats impacting SGCN and the necessary conservation actions to address those threats, the second-level threats were determined to be sufficient for adequately identifying those threats, while the second-level conservation actions were determined to be insufficient for adequately identifying the needed conservation actions. In order to address this issue, while still utilizing the standardized conservation actions, third-level conservation actions were developed. The third-level data gaps were established by summarizing the conservation actions identified in the 2015 AWAP and then sorting them into the second-level conservation actions. These third-level conservation actions can then be rolled up to the second or first-level conservation actions. Since standardized data gaps have not been established, and to maintain this standardized approach, we developed a two-tiered list of data gaps or research needs. The first-level data gaps were modeled after Texas Parks and Wildlife Department's approach, mentioned above, while the second-level data gaps were established by summarizing the data gaps or research needs identified in the 2015 AWAP and then sorting them into the first-level For this revision, the terrestrial and aquatic Habitat Teams reassessed the habitat sections of this plan and decided to update the terrestrial and aquatic habitat types to create a more structured and informative approach to identifying and managing the various habitat types found in the state. This includes the introduction of thirteen habitat Guilds, forty-two updated AWAP habitat types, and eight new Sub-aquatic habitat types, which are further split into various Micro-aquatic Habitat Types, each nesting within the former to create a structured, hierarchical approach to classifying these habitat types (Chapter 3: Table 
3
.1, 3.2, 3.3, 3.4). See Chapter 3 for descriptions of and more information about the AWAP, Sub-aquatic, and Micro-aquatic habitat types and Guilds.
Taxa teams then worked to classify the level of association or dependency of each SGCN on every habitat type. Species habitat associations were classified as either obligate, optimal, suitable, associated, unsuitable, or unknown (Table 
2
.6). Aquatic habitat types associations were not made for terrestrial species.
management and conservation actions. Also, if changes need to be made to adapt ongoing conservation efforts to respond appropriately to new information or changing conditions. This information is essential to making effective management decisions and evaluating the outcomes of those decisions.
Information will be obtained when possible from various conservation partners, researchers, projects, initiatives, such as community science efforts (e.g., eBird, iNaturalist, Master Naturalist Programs, etc.), and any other available sources to help monitor species' responses, trends, and other valuable information. Arkansas remains committed to obtaining population and management response data, when possible, and using that information and other available knowledge and expertise to make science-based decisions to inform management actions and update management plans to further the conservation of these species and their habitats.
Taxa Summary: Mammals Mammals are a highly diverse group of species that can be found in various habitats across the entire state, from large elk and bears down to small bats and shrews. The Ozark region is probably the most diverse due to the many thousands of caves that provide habitat for bat species.The class Mammalia has 67 species in 9 different orders in Arkansas. The most diverse order is Rodentia (rodents) with 28 species followed by bats (Chiroptera) with 16 species. In addition to the many native mammals in Arkansas, there are a few troublesome invasive species, such as nutria, black rats, and house mice. Sixteen of the native species (24%) are considered species of greatest conservation need; 7 bats, 5 rodents, 2 shrews, and 2 carnivores. Birds are a very diverse and highly-mobile group of species that can be found all across Arkansas.
Although, the occurrence and distribution of many of these species varies by season. Most of Arkansas's bird species are found only in specific habitats or particular ecoregions or can only found in Arkansas during certain times of year depending on their status (i.e., resident, migrant, breeding, wintering, and accidentals). While there are species that occupy Arkansas year-round, there are no bird species that are considered to be endemic to Arkansas.
There are 11,000 living bird species worldwide, with just under 4,000 bird species in the Western Hemisphere. There are 2,069 bird species found in North America. The exact number can vary slightly depending on the source and how species are classified. In Arkansas, over400 species of
The rich botanical heritage of Arkansas is under pressure from a variety of interconnected threats. Human-induced changes to the landscape, the introduction of aggressive non-native species, and the disruption of natural ecological processes like fire, have placed many native plant species and communities at risk. To conserve our native plants, efforts should be made to protect and restore habitats on both public and private lands. Invasive species treatment and eradication will be an important component of habitat restoration to reduce competition with native plants. Efforts to monitor the status of rare plants and understand reasons for declines will be important for effective conservation.
In Arkansas, authority over native plants resides with the Arkansas Natural Heritage Commission (ANHC). The ANHC tracks the occurrence of 617 vascular plants species that it considers to be of state conservation concern and serves as the central repository for data on rare plants and natural communities. ANHC uses this occurrence data in conjunction with available information on threats and population trends, often consulting with experts on particular taxa, to determine and monitor the status of rare plant species within Arkansas. In addition to tracking rare species occurrences, ANHC manages a system of Natural Areas that protects high quality examples of important natural communities, and maintains an herbarium with over 20,000 accessioned plant specimens.
Plant conservation in Arkansas is a collaborative effort, with botanists, biologists, and land managers from federal, state, university, and non-profit organizations working together and along with the public to monitor species and manage habitat for plants. The addition of plants to the wildlife action plan serves as a formal platform to help prioritize actions and build additional collaboration for plant conservation.
Taxa Team Members: Allison Fowler (AGFC), Ben Benton (ANHC), Theo Witsell (Southeastern Turbidity and total suspended solid concentrations are usually low except in the Red River.
Summer flow in many small streams is limited or nonexistent but enduring pools may occur.
Waters tend to be stained by organics, thus lowering water clarity and increasing total organic carbon and biochemical oxygen demand levels. Most streams have a sandy substrate and a forest canopy. Many do not flow during the summer or early fall. However, in sandhills, springfed, perennial streams occur; here, total dissolved solids, total suspended solids, alkalinity and hardness values are lower than elsewhere in this ecoregion. Water quality in forested basins is better than in pastureland. Oil production has lowered stream quality in the south.
Water quality in forested watersheds tends to be good and is better than in pastureland. Today, much of the historic mesic forest is intact, where the dissected topography prevented most conversion attempts. Flatter areas have been converted to pasture and hay meadow, with urbanization important in some areas.

## St. Francis River Basin

The St. Francis River system drains the smallest region of the state, only 4,200 sq. mi. of northeast Arkansas. Due to extensive channelization in the 1950s, it is primarily a turbid, lowland stream and bears little physical resemblance to the stream that was described in 1896 (Meek 1896). There is little or no vegetation, and substrates consist largely of mud, sand, and, very infrequently, small gravel areas near sandbars.

## Habitat Types

The habitat teams described the habitats of Arkansas in 42 AWAP habitat types in Table 
3
.1.
Native habitat types in Arkansas were adapted from definitions provided by NatureServe (2005).
In addition, un-natural or man-made habitats that can mimic native habitats and can support species for part of their life history were included (types marked with an asterisk in Table 
3
.1).
For each native terrestrial habitat type, the terrestrial habitat team developed a brief description of the type as well as information on spatial ecology, condition, and a set of desired future conditions. This information is intended for use by natural resource managers in planning, creating, restoring, and maintaining native communities. The detailed information for each habitat type is located in Appendix B. For aquatic habitats, the aquatic habitat team identified sub-and micro-habitat types that are nested under the broader types (Table 
3
.2). 

## AWAP Habitat Guilds

In order to provide a more streamlined approach, communicate better with the public, and align with other state wildlife action plans, the AWAP habitat types were placed into coarse habitat guilds (Table 
3
.3). The thirteen guilds were adapted from the list Coarse Habitat Categories developed by Chrisfield and the Northeast Wildlife Diversity Technical Committee (2022) and further modified for use in the Southeast region. Guilds provide a general overview of the habitat type and simplify information for communicating with a broad audience. Table 
3
.4
illustrates the hierarchical structure of the guilds and habitat types.

These wetlands may occur in poorly developed upland drainages, toe-slopes, and small headwaters stream bottoms. These environments are prone to long duration standing water, and tend to occur on highly acidic, nutrient-poor soils. The vegetation is characterized by sweetbay magnolia, blackgum, swamp tupelo, and red maple. The understory consistently supports an abundance of ferns.

## Prairies and Savannas

Prairies are open grasslands dominated by herbaceous plants (grasses and forbs) with little woody vegetation. Savannas are often a gradient between prairie and woodland and are characterized by few trees (less than 30% canopy cover) and a lush understory of grasses and forbs. The primary native warm season grasses found in prairies and savannas are little bluestem, switchgrass, big bluestem, and Indiangrass. In addition, this system is host to a variety of flowering plants.
Ozarks and Ouachitas: The shale-derived soils associated with the Arkansas Valley prairies are thin and droughty. The prairies of the Ozark Highlands occur on level to gently rolling areas underlain by limestone and chert, and soils are also thin and droughty. In the Ouachitas, prairies are generally restricted to the tops of dry, sandstone ridges. The combined effect of droughty soils, reduced precipitation, and prevailing level topography create conditions highly conducive to the ignition and spread of fires. Remaining prairies in this region are small and isolated.
West Gulf Coastal Plain: In the West Gulf Coastal Plain of southwest Arkansas, prairies occur over relatively deep calcareous soils and are known as blackland prairie. These high-clay content, shrink-swell soils resist invasion by woody species, which combined with fire, maintains the prairie, savanna, and open woodlands.
Mississippi Alluvial Plain: Prairie in east-central Arkansas, known as the Grand Prairie, historically covered over 320,000 acres. The Grand Prairie is wetter than most prairies, receiving up to 50 inches of rain per year. This system of prairies and woodlands occurs on the oldest substantial land surfaces in the Mississippi River Alluvial Valley and the highest land surface in the riverdeposited portions of the Mississippi Alluvial Plain ecoregion. It occupies a very flat region up to 20 miles wide and 60 miles long bounded by present day rivers, especially the Arkansas and White, which are much lower in elevation than the Grand Prairie terrace. This terrace is covered with thin soils underlain by deep layers of impervious clay. Although productive, these soils are droughty due to the impervious clay subsoils. The combination of droughty soils, very flat topography, and the lack of major stream corridors in the region create conditions suitable to the ignition and spread of fires. Almost annual fires would have been necessary to maintain these prairies.
Historically, the landscape of Arkansas included more than 2,000,000 acres of open grasslands, including tallgrass prairie. Today, less than 1 percent of native prairie remains. Land development and conversion have played a significant role in the decline. The scattered remnants of prairie that remain are fragmented and often degraded by lack of prescribed fire.

## Glades and Barrens

## Glades

Glades are open, rocky areas typically embedded in upland woodlands in the Ozarks and Ouachitas. They have thin soils, few trees, and areas of exposed rock. There are over 55,000 acres of glades in Arkansas. These habitats are hugely dependent upon fire and without it become choked with eastern red cedar. There are many species of plants and animals adapted to this hot and dry environment, including several rare plant species. In Arkansas, there are seven distinct types of glades that are distinguished by their underlying rock type and soil chemistry, but in general, glades can be separated into either limestone or sandstone glades.
Limestone or calcareous glades are found on south or west-facing moderate to steep slopes.
Limestone, dolomite or shale bedrock typify this system with shallow, moderately to welldrained soils interspersed with rocks. These soils often dry out during the summer and autumn, and then become saturated during the winter and spring. Little bluestem dominates this system and is commonly associated with big bluestem, sideoats grama, and calcium-loving plant species.
Stunted woodlands primarily dominated by chinkapin oak interspersed with eastern red cedar occur on variable-depth-to-bedrock soils.
Sandstone glades are found on moderate to steep slopes or valley walls of rivers along most aspects. Parent material includes chert, shale and/or sandstone bedrock with well-to excessively well-drained, shallow soils interspersed with rock and boulders. These soils are typically dry during the summer and autumn, becoming saturated during the spring and winter. Grasses such as little bluestem and Indiangrass dominate this system with stunted oak species such as post oak, blackjack oak and shrub species such as blueberry/farkleberry (Vaccinium spp). This system is influenced by drought and infrequent to occasional fires.

## Barrens

Barrens are the result of extreme soil conditions and occur predominantly in the West Gulf Coastal Plain. The saline barrens occur on soils with a high level of sodium and magnesium salts. This can inhibit plants from growing, particularly woody species, resulting in what looks to be a barren field. However, there are some plant species adapted to this extreme condition, including the rare geocarpon. Other typical plant species may include post oak, blackjack oak, bottomland post oak, saltbush, barberry hawthorn, three-awn grasses, little bluestem, longspike tridens, and rose bluet. Saline barrens often occur embedded within pine flatwood systems.
In southwest Arkansas, interspersed within West Gulf Coastal Plain sandhill oak and shortleaf pine-oak forest and woodlands are the sandhill barrens. This community occurs on deep, excessively well-drained sands. Sandhill barrens are typically open, sparsely wooded areas that have drought-adapted, fire-tolerant herbaceous vegetation and patches of bare soil. Dominant species include Arkansas oak, shortleaf pine, several species of three-awn grass (Aristida spp.), little bluestem, splitbeard bluestem, slender Indiangrass, and many species of forbs (some of which are rare). Arkansas' Designated Scenic and Wild Rivers Several of Arkansas' rivers have been recognized for their pristine nature and ecological significance. The Buffalo National River, the first federally protected national river in the United States, is renowned for its clear waters, towering bluffs, and rich biodiversity. Other notable rivers include the Cossatot River, known for its rugged terrain and whitewater rapids, and the Spring River, a spring-fed system with consistently cold, clear water supporting trout and other aquatic life.
Big Piney Creek, Buffalo River, Cossatot River, Hurricane Creek, Little Missouri River, Mulberry River, North Sylamore Creek, Richland Creek are recognized by the National Wild and Scenic River System. Approximately 210 miles of streams and rivers are designated as Wild and Scenic in Arkansas, and all of these waterways are found in the Ozark and Ouachita Mountain ecoregions.
Ozark and Ouachita Mountain Streams: The Ozark and Ouachita Mountains of Arkansas are characterized by high gradient, fast-flowing, clear-water streams with gravel-pebble substrates and defined riffle-run-pool mesohabitats. These streams, often spring-fed, maintain relatively cool temperatures year-round, providing essential habitat for freshwater species. Vegetation along these streams includes riparian hardwood forests featuring sycamore, river birch, and sweetgum trees. Water willow is a common instream vegetation type present. The stability and high water quality of these systems make them vital for sustaining aquatic biodiversity.
A key difference between streams in the Ozarks and Ouachitas is their alkalinity and conductivity with streams in the Ozarks have catchments high in limestone therefore the waters tend to have higher conductivity and more alkaline. The Ozarks are also characterized by their extensive karst habitat including over 3,000 springs and numerous caves that are home to many cave-adapted aquatic species. Characteristic waterways in the Ozarks include White River, Buffalo River, Kings River, Mulberry River, Illinois River, and Spring River.
In contrast streams in the Ouachitas have catchments high in sandstone (less limestone) and waters that are lower in conductivity and neutral to slightly acidic. Characteristic waterways in the Ouachitas include the upper Ouachita River, upper Saline River, Little Missouri River, and the Caddo River.
Mississippi Alluvial Plain Rivers: The rivers of the Mississippi Alluvial Plain contrast sharply with the Ozark and Ouachita streams. These slow-moving, meandering rivers are often turbid with soft, silty bottoms and low stream gradient. Key examples include the lower White River, Cache River, St. Francis River, Bayou Bartholomew, and Arkansas River, which support vast floodplain ecosystems, bottomland hardwood forests, and wetland communities. These waters are vital for migratory birds, including prothonotary warblers, wood ducks, and several species of waterfowl.
Seasonal flooding enriches these systems, making them some of the most biologically productive areas in the state. For example, the Cache-Lower White River bottomland ecosystem is recognized as a Wetland of International Importance.
West Gulf Coastal Plain Rivers: The West Gulf Coastal Plain, located in southern and southwestern Arkansas, features low gradient, slow-moving, tannin-stained rivers and streams that flow through extensive pine and bottomland hardwood forests. These rivers, such as the lower Saline, Little Missouri, and lower Ouachita rivers, often have sandy or muddy substrates and support a diverse mix of aquatic and terrestrial species. The West Gulf Coastal Plain rivers are ecologically significant because of their connectivity to large floodplain ecosystems and cypress-tupelo swamps. Blackwater streams, which drain forested wetlands, are particularly characteristic of this region and host species such as American alligators, Paddlefish, and various freshwater mussels.
Arkansas Valley Rivers and Streams: The Arkansas Valley, situated between the Ozark and Ouachita Mountains, is defined by the Arkansas River, one of the most significant waterways in the state. The river's course through Arkansas has been heavily modified by locks and dams for navigation, altering its natural hydrology. However, portions of the river and its tributaries still provide important stream and wetland habitats. The Petit Jean, Mulberry, and Fourche La Fave rivers are key tributaries that sustain diverse aquatic life. This region features a mix of high-energy river channels, oxbow lakes, and seasonally flooded bottomlands, making it an important area for migratory shorebirds, amphibians, and aquatic turtles. The river's backwaters and tributaries also serve as nursery areas for fish and other aquatic organisms.
Arkansas' rivers and streams are essential to the state's ecology, economy, and recreation.
Whether providing clean drinking water, sustaining diverse aquatic life, or offering scenic beauty for canoeing and fishing, these waterways remain a cornerstone of Arkansas' natural heritage.

## Reservoirs

Arkansas is home to an extensive array of lakes and ponds, providing essential aquatic habitat, recreation, and water resources across the state (Figure 
3
.4). These standing waterbodies exhibit a diversity of hydrological, geological, and ecological characteristics that vary across Arkansas' different ecoregions. The state's lakes and reservoirs are typically categorized based on their origin (natural or artificial), water clarity, depth, trophic status, and surrounding landscape.
Arkansas features several distinct lake types, including oxbow lakes of the Mississippi Alluvial Plain, deep clear reservoirs of the Ozarks and Ouachitas, and numerous small farm ponds and recreational lakes across the West Gulf Coastal Plain and Arkansas Valley.
Lakes and ponds in Arkansas provide critical habitat for a wide array of aquatic plants, fish, and wildlife species. Many species of greatest conservation need (SGCN) rely on these lentic systems, including the Alligator Gar, Fat Pocketbook Mussel, and Western Chicken Turtle. Several lakes in Arkansas are designated as Extraordinary Resource Waters (ERWs) by the Arkansas Department of Environmental Quality (ADEQ). This designation is granted to water bodies with exceptional ecological, recreational, and aesthetic value, ensuring their long-term protection. 

## Arkansas' Designated Natural and Scenic Lakes

Although Arkansas is primarily dominated by man-made reservoirs, several natural lakes have been recognized for their pristine nature and ecological significance. Lake Chicot, the largest oxbow lake in North America, is known for its historic meander pattern, abundant fisheries, and migratory waterfowl habitat. Other notable lakes include Horseshoe Lake, Big Lake, and Grassy Lake, which are remnants of past Mississippi River courses and provide vital floodplain habitat for fish and wildlife.
Ozark and Ouachita Highland Reservoirs: The Ozark and Ouachita Mountain ecoregions are home to deep, clear reservoirs that were formed by damming major rivers for flood control, hydropower, and recreation. These lakes have rocky or gravel substrates, high water clarity, and well-oxygenated depths, creating ideal conditions for various species.
These lakes are characterized by steep, forested shorelines, deep water zones, and a mix of rocky and sandy substrates. The high water quality and stable conditions of these reservoirs make them ideal for fish spawning, recreational boating, and water supply.
Large reservoirs in the Ozarks typically discharge hypolimnetic waters resulting in tailwaters that are cold and characterized by lower dissolved oxygen. Tailwaters of these reservoirs are often stocked with trout.
Mississippi Alluvial Plain Lakes and Oxbows: The Mississippi Alluvial Plain (Delta) features oxbow lakes, floodplain lakes, and backwater sloughs that are remnants of ancient river meanders.
These shallow, nutrient-rich waterbodies support dense aquatic vegetation, abundant fish populations, and seasonal waterfowl migrations.
These lakes often have soft, silty bottoms, warm temperatures, and tannin-stained waters.
Seasonal flooding connects them to adjacent rivers, allowing for fish migrations and wetland recharge.
West Gulf Coastal Plain Lakes and Ponds: The West Gulf Coastal Plain, located in southern and southwestern Arkansas, contains numerous small lakes, ponds, and reservoirs that support aquatic ecosystems within pine forests, bottomland hardwoods, and wetlands.
Lakes in this region often have tannic, slightly acidic waters with sand or silt substrates.
Arkansas River Valley Reservoirs and Backwaters: The Arkansas River Valley is heavily influenced by the Arkansas River Navigation System, which has transformed the natural flow regime of the river and created numerous impoundments and oxbows.
The seasonal hydrology and connectivity of these reservoirs with floodplains create important habitat for aquatic life and waterfowl.
Arkansas' lakes and ponds serve as critical ecosystems, recreational destinations, and water supply sources. These habitats support a wide range of aquatic species, provide economic benefits, and sustain biodiversity across the state. However, anthropogenic pressures, habitat degradation, and climate variability threaten their ecological integrity. Continued conservation efforts, habitat restoration, and sustainable management will be essential in preserving Arkansas' freshwater heritage for future generations.

## Cropland and Pastureland

Cropland in Arkansas is located mostly in the Mississippi Alluvial Plain and includes predominantly soybeans, rice, and cotton. The monoculture coupled with extensive chemical use does not provide much wildlife habitat, though edges around fields may offer habitat diversity.
Partially flooded rice and soybean fields can offer stopover habitat for migrating shorebirds, wading birds, and ducks.
Pastureland includes land with mixed grasses or monocultures of non-native grasses managed to support grazing domestic mammals. Many pastures were established on historical prairies.
Pastures occur statewide in Arkansas, but are less common in the Mississippi Alluvial Plain and Crowley's Ridge. Pastures reduce the availability of more suitable habitats on the landscape for species of conservation concern. Although, the endemic Ozark pocket gopher is known almost exclusively from pasture ground.

## Plantation

Plantations in Arkansas are primarily composed of loblolly pine with regularly spaced trees planted for commercial production and subject to periodic thinning and cutting. Pine plantations are extensive in the West Gulf Coastal Plain and Ouachitas, but can occur statewide. They can be used by species of conservation concern, particularly songbirds during migration or quail shortly after planting and thinning. However, the intense management coupled with the absence of fire, often results in a lack of midstory or understory required by many species.

## Developed Areas

Developed areas are industrial, urban, or suburban. This includes buildings surrounded by pavement, short grass, shrubs and open-grown trees, interspersed with parkland and commercial areas. High concentrations of non-native and/or invasive plants are commonly associated with urban areas. Little cover may be available for wildlife, as often lawns are managed with a monoculture of manicured, non-native grass. Native gardens can provide resources for pollinators, small mammals, lizards, and birds. 

## Conservation Actions

The primary conservation actions to mitigate stated threats for habitats are protection, restoration, management, and education. High-level conservation actions needed for habitats that will benefit SGCN are to:
• Identify priority areas for protection and conservation.
• Protect priority areas through land acquisition or easements.
• Restore degraded sites through a combination of forest management, prescribed fire, native plantings, and invasive species removals.
• Implement an appropriate prescribed fire regime to maintain fire-dependent habitats at desired condition.
• Educate and engage private landowners on habitat management actions.
• Incentivize habitat management and restoration on private lands.

## Monitoring

Monitoring the extent and condition of our native habitats over time is important in understanding how much suitable habitat is available to SGCN and how our management actions are impacting habitats. Monitoring of habitats typically occurs at 3 spatial scales: project level, site level, and landscape level.

## Project Level

All habitat restoration projects funded through state wildlife grants include a monitoring component to determine effectiveness of activities toward improving habitat condition. Projects also include commitments by managers to maintain habitat for a determined period of time.

## Site Level

The AGFC's habitat program manages over 350,000 acres across its wildlife management areas (WMAs), with 300,000 acres certified under the Sustainable Forestry Initiative® Forest Management Standard. To assess current conditions and management effectiveness, forest inventories are conducted in delineated stands on WMAs every 3-10 years. The collected metrics enable managers to assign the appropriate AWAP habitat type and measure its condition, indicating whether it is at the desired state (using criteria provided in Appendix B). This data also allows for the comparison of habitat type and condition changes over time on AGFC owned areas. As areas are evaluated, managers discuss and adapt prescriptions to meet habitat objectives.

## Landscape Level

Monitoring habitats on a large scale can be difficult with limited resources. Often, GIS data or mapping layers are used to help fill in gaps and provide a broader picture of habitat extents.
Arkansas worked with the Missouri Resource Assessment Partnership to map Arkansas habitats at a fine scale (<10m resolution). One hundred and twenty-three ecological mapping systems were mapped across the state using information on slope, soils, aspect, and dominant vegetation (identified through ground-truthing). These systems were then cross-walked to the AWAP habitat types, and subsequently the habitat guilds, providing us with a cover map for the state 
(Figure 3.5)
. This allows us to understand where habitats are located in the state and the amount of each. The mapping technique used to create this map is replicable. Therefore, in 5 or 10 years we can compare habitat extents to determine where we have gains or losses in habitat types. On conservation lands and/or sensitive habitats, additional monitoring can be done to determine habitat condition. Landscape conditions were assessed using an update to the SE Conservation Blueprint for Arkansas (Gray and Jones-Farrand 2019). For terrestrial habitat systems, Habitat Condition Index scores for all 30-m pixels in the Blueprint were averaged within a 2,471-ac hexagon grid generated for the State of Arkansas. For aquatic systems, Habitat Condition Index scores were calculated for 12-digit Hydrologic Unit Code (HUC12) watersheds. In both cases, the Landscape Condition Index was calculated by binning the range of scores across the state into High (3), Moderate (2) or Low (1) categories. In the case of terrestrial systems, certain rare plant communities (e.g., saline glades) were identified as conservation priorities and any landscape containing 1 or more of those communities was automatically assigned a Landscape Condition Index value of High (3).
Species status was assessed using location data for 298 Species of Greatest Conservation Need (SGCN) as identified in the 2015 AWAP. These species covered a wide array of taxa groups, including plants, invertebrates, mussels, crayfish, fish, reptiles, amphibians, birds and mammals.
The species were categorized into terrestrial or aquatic divisions, and location data were gathered from a variety of sources. Location data were then summarized to the hexagon or HUC12 landscapes using a weighted sum approach. Each species in each landscape was weighed by the age of its most recent observation such that more recent observations carried more weight than older observations. After weighting, species counts were summed. The Species Condition Index was calculated by binning the range of scores across the state into High (3), Moderate (2) or Low (1) categories. Similar to the Landscape Condition Index, species identified as Narrow Range Endemics by taxa experts were identified as conservation priorities and any landscape containing 1 or more of those communities was automatically assigned a Species Condition Index value of High (3).

## Spatial priority areas identified by conservation and other organizations can provide information

on the relative effort focused on a landscape and thereby indicate opportunities to leverage partnerships and amplify resources to achieve goals. The Conservation Opportunity Index was calculated by overlaying spatial priorities for ten national, regional, and state-wide organizations and binning the range of scores across the state into High (3), Moderate (2) or Low (1) categories.
The 3 indices were combined in a "bar code" approach (e.g., HHH). The bar codes were then sorted into 13 classes based on combinations of interest to the team 
(Figures 4.1)
. In a subsequent refinement, the 13 classes were simplified to 5 color codes on the maps that indicated similar groups of conservation actions (e.g. maintenance or restoration). Chapter 5: Arkansas: A regional perspective
In 2018, the Association of Fish and Wildlife Agencies (AFWA) adopted a resolution on landscape conservation that recognized "the importance of collaborating at landscape scales to help fish and wildlife agencies meet their statutory and regulatory responsibilities to conserve fish and wildlife and their habitats." In response to the resolution, AFWA established a President's Task Force on Shared Science & Landscape Conservation Priorities in 2020, which recommended that State Wildlife Action Plans (SWAPs) serve as a framework for regional coordination and collaboration.
Within this Plan, Arkansas has identified Species of Greatest Conservation Need (SGCN) and outlined strategies to sustain them, including conservation actions to promote species recovery and prevent federal listings under the Endangered Species Act. To sustain the species that are the responsibility of Arkansas to protect and that also reflect the rich biodiversity, culture, and history of the state, Arkansas must consider its role and the influences of the larger Southeast landscape.
This chapter serves to examine regional, landscape-scale considerations for Arkansas and a means for the state to find potential collaborations across the Southeast to best support the state's SGCN and regional species of greatest conservation need (RSGCN). Additionally, some of the threats that impact SGCN (e.g., climate change impacts), as well as the residents and local economies of the state, are regional and local. Addressing these threats effectively requires aligning conservation strategies across state boundaries. By using consistent regional information, shared by other states to inform their own SWAPs, Arkansas may be able to better address its contributions to regional conservation priorities, identify potential landscape level threats, and help connect our region's lands and waters.
Arkansas's Conservation Portfolio: Connecting the Region's Lands and Waters
Arkansas supports a wide diversity of habitats, culturally and historically significant landscapes, and ecosystems that provide benefits to the state as well as the broader Southeast region. It plays an important role in connecting the lands and waters of the Southeast Region, as well as hosting some regionally important ecosystems and habitat types like natural prairies and shortleaf pine. Many regional and local partners and partnerships are working with the Arkansas Game and Fish Commission (AGFC) to help conserve the state's iconic and important landscapes.
Beyond planning, management of state resources requires working beyond the borders on a regular basis. Many of the landscapes of state lands along the border extend across the border and most SGCN have ranges beyond the borders of Arkansas, requiring partnerships with neighboring states and regional or international partners to accomplish needed actions to achieve shared goals. Watersheds and flyways often encompass all or parts of multiple states and require complex coordination among many partners and jurisdictions to improve conditions, regulate harvest and methods, and provide needed habitat. Recovery of declining species that have large ranges requires coordination with partners and agencies with interest and jurisdiction throughout the species' range.
Much of this work may include efforts to protect and maintain migratory species habitat throughout their annual cycles, which is called full life-cycle conservation. Full life-cycle conservation of migratory species is one area of Arkansas's commitment to cross-border conservation and is imperative to the improvement and long-term sustainability of Arkansas's natural communities and species.
In addition to contributing to the conservation landscape of the Southeast, Arkansas's lands and waters also benefit the state's economy. The Bureau of Economic Analysis (BEA) provides
Outdoor Recreation Satellite Accounts for each state. In 2022, the BEA estimated that the outdoor recreation economy generated $4.1 billion in value for the state's Gross Domestic Product and another $ 1.9 billion in wages and salaries.

## Arkansas and the Southeast Conservation Adaptation Strategy (SECAS)

The Southeast Conservation Adaptation Strategy (SECAS) is a regional conservation initiative that spans the Southeastern United States and Caribbean. SECAS brings together diverse partners around an ambitious goal: a 10% or greater improvement in the health, function, and connectivity of Southeastern ecosystems by 2060. The Southeast Conservation Blueprint (v2024)
is the primary product of SECAS and is a living, spatial plan that identifies priority areas where conservation action would make an impact, based on a suite of natural and cultural resource indicators, towards creating a connected networks of lands and waters across the Southeast.
Over 1,700 people from more than 500 different organizations have been actively involved in developing the Blueprint for achieving a connected network of lands and waters across the region.
The Blueprint recognizes more than 17.5 million acres, or roughly 52% of the state, as a priority for connecting the region's lands and waters (Table 
5
.1 and Figure 
5
.1). About 9.2 million acres (27%) are rated as highest or high priority, meaning that these are areas where conservation action would make the biggest impact, based on a suite of natural and cultural resource indicators, towards a connected network of lands and waters. An additional 6.1 million acres (18.7%) are rated as medium priority. These are areas where conservation action would make an above-average impact, based on a suite of natural and cultural resource indicators. There are also about 2.3 million acres (6.7%) that are considered a priority connection, or areas that can help facilitate the flow of species and ecological processes within the state while also considering connectivity with the broader region. One aspect to consider when characterizing the landscape of Arkansas is through EPA level III ecoregions. Evaluation of ecoregions that Arkansas shares with its neighbors can provide a potential framework to assess and compare ecosystems and ecosystem components as these areas are similar regarding their respective mosaics of biotic, abiotic, terrestrial, and aquatic ecosystems. Below we show a table of the ecoregions of Arkansas and how much of each ecoregion within Arkansas is identified in the Southeast Blueprint (Table 
5
.2). 

## Arkansas and the SECAS goal

Through SECAS, partners work together to design and achieve a connected network for the benefit of ecosystems, species, and people. The long-term goal for SECAS is a 10% or greater improvement in the health, function, and connectivity of Southeastern ecosystems by 2060. To stay on track for achieving that goal, a 1% improvement will be needed every 4 years. While the primary product of SECAS is the Southeast Conservation Blueprint, which serves as the framework to achieve this goal, there is also an ongoing effort to track and report on regional ecosystem metrics. The analysis presented in Table 
3
 shows the overall trends within the Southeast, however the 2024 goal report also shows additional data that is more specific to Arkansas inputs to these trends. Arkansas has seen slight increases in natural landcover in floodplains and over 52% increases in conservation practices on working land acres, but also shows declining trends in certain habitat types and ecosystem functions such as water quality.

## Arkansas ecoregions

Regional Species of Greatest Conservation Need and produce a list of 960 regional priority species (1,034 including subspecies), or animal RSGCN and also a report that outlines the process and results. This effort aligns with the Northeast Association of Fish and Wildlife Agencies' similar project to develop a RSGCN list for the Northeast Region. Out of the total animal RSGCN list, two-thirds are freshwater fish, crayfish, and freshwater mussels.
Identifying animal RSGCN drew upon a collaborative process among Southeastern State fish and wildlife agencies and partners that involved more than 100 experts and used a set of consistent criteria to review current scientific information and evaluate state-identified SGCN. Species were evaluated based on several primary factors, including: 1) the level of conservation concern (i.e. extinction risk), 2) regional stewardship responsibility (i.e. importance of the Southeast in conservation of the species), and 3) biological or ecological significance (e.g., unique evolutionary lineages). The regional assessment focused on species in key taxonomic groups, including vertebrates (mammals, birds, reptiles, amphibians, and fishes) as well as several betterknown groups of invertebrate animals. Scientific experts in each of these groups convened to evaluate and identify those species that warranted identification as a regional priority.
Additionally, the science teams characterized the level of conservation concern for each regional priority, ranging from moderate to high and very high concern. Through this established process, the RSGCN list can be updated as the States' SGCN lists change over time.
The number of RSGCN that are considered the stewardship responsibilities of each state varies widely. These patterns reflect the underlying diversity of species in each state-particularly the number of imperiled and/or limited range species-which is also influenced by a state's size and diversity of habitats. These patterns are also influenced by the "regional stewardship responsibility" criterion in the assessment of priority species.
Nearly 70% of regional priority species are endemic to the 15-state SEAFWA region. Overall, more than half (55%) of RSGCN are shared by three or more states, presenting opportunities for cross-state conservation collaboration. The remaining 45% of RSGCN have narrow ranges and are found in just one or two states. By taxa, fish and crayfish represent 47% of all RSGCN within the region. Many aquatic habitats and ecosystems within the Southeast are highly fragmented, resulting in severe limitations to ecosystem integrity and species persistence regionwide. Habitat fragmentation in rivers and streams is a determining factor in the decline in abundance of numerous species of fishes, freshwater mussels, and amphibians such as Ozark hellbender.
Figure 
5
.3. Species of Greatest Conservation Need (SGCN) and Regional SGCN (RSGCN) within each state and territory. Puerto Rico and the US Virgin Islands were not included in the RSGCN effort but do support some of the species or are within the range of some RSGCNs, which is why the numbers of RSGCN are much lower in these territories. To develop a pool of potential species to draw from, the PCA worked with NatureServe to compile a list of more than 10,000 vascular plants native to any of the SEAFWA considering each species' G-Rank (global rarity), S-Rank (state rarity) and endemism. Unfortunately, due to data limitations, plants native to Puerto Rico and the U.S. Virgin Islands could not be included.
The full list received extensive review from botany experts, a technical team composed of representatives from each state, and NatureServe. Based on partner feedback, the technical team assigned each species a level of conservation concern rating ranging from low to very high or "manual review needed". Any plant scoring moderate or above was ultimately considered an RSGCN. After cleaning the data to remove redundancies, the complete Southeastern plant list evaluated 9,271 species and prioritized 1,824 as RSGCN. 72% of the plant RSGCN are endemic to the Southeast. Many of these same habitats tend to provide crucial habitat for animal RSGCN as well, conserving these ecosystems can safeguard regional biodiversity more broadly. Arkansas is also home to multiple species that are not only endemic to the SEAFWA region but are also found in only one other state. For example, Arkansas and Missouri support 1 fish, 5 crayfish, 3 amphibians and 1 mollusk that are RSGCN and only found within these two states.
There are also 5 species of crayfish, 1 mollusk, and 1 amphibian found only in Louisiana and Arkansas. Additionally, there are 2 species of amphibians, 4 species of crayfish, and 5 types of fishes whose range only occur within Arkansas and Oklahoma.
By identifying shared RSGCN, Arkansas can prioritize conservation actions that not only provide local benefits but support a regional landscape. For example, Arkansas can identify conservation strategies aimed at improving aquatic connectivity that also improve water quantity and quality around watersheds and streams that cut across neighboring states.

## Terrestrial Resilience

Much of Arkansas's natural landscape is considered a resilient terrestrial site. The Nature Conservancy (TNC) developed a data set called the Resilient Land Data and identifies areas with Highly resilient sites contain many different habitat niches that support biodiversity and allow species to move freely through the landscape to find suitable microclimates as the climate changes. Resilience emphasizes diverse landscapes where species are likely to be able to move and adjust to changing conditions. While the species themselves may change, these areas are likely to be able to host a continued diversity of species into the future. Currently the Southeast Conservation Blueprint's analysis of the state rates 65.9% of the acreage as average resilience or above (Table 
5
.4, Figure 
5
.7). 

## Working with Partners Across the Region

There are multiple regional partnerships that include the state of Arkansas within their conservation planning. Below, we describe opportunities for Arkansas to rally conservation partners to shared areas of interest and describe on-going collaborations. Multiple bird species of conservation concern within the LMVJV footprint are also listed as shared RSGCN between multiple states with actions being pursued. Additional focus on the habitat supporting these species could prove to be beneficial to not only these species but additional associated species as well. Currently there are 11 species of birds on Oklahoma's SGCN list that are also identified as a priority by the LMVJV.

## Birds of Conservation

## Central Hardwoods Joint Venture

The Central Hardwoods Joint Venture (CHJV) boundaries include portions of 6 states and operates within the Ozark Highlands of northern Arkansas extending down to the Ouachita Mountains. Like the LMVJV, the CHJV is a collaborative partnership including private landowners, Non-Governmental Organizations (NGO), state, and federal agencies focused on priority bird habitat and conservation. The CHJV recognizes the importance of ecosystem conservation and restoration as a critical component in supporting important habitat for priority bird populations.
Currently, there are 16 species which Arkansas recognizes as SGCN that are also recognized as priority species by the CHJV.

## Southeast Aquatic Resources Partnership

The Southeast Aquatic Resources Partnership (SARP) is a regional collaboration of natural resource and science agencies, conservation organizations and private interests developed to strengthen the management and conservation of aquatic resources in the southeastern United
States. SARP is an official subcommittee of SEAFWA that aims to support and facilitate on-theground and in-the-water science-based action to improve and protect aquatic habitats and resources.
To reverse the unprecedented decline in Southeastern aquatic resources, SARP utilizes sciencebased programs to coordinate conservation and restoration activities. SARP programs cover a range of aquatic resource interests including coastal restoration, native Black Bass and other native fish conservation areas, flow alterations, and aquatic connectivity. SARP works to coordinate biologists, practitioners, recreational paddlers, and a myriad of other interested parties by establishing Aquatic Connectivity Teams (ACTs) across the Southeast. ACTs are statebased teams that are developed upon request from interested state partners. Teams make barrier removal projects a reality by combining effort across organizations and jurisdictions and working collaboratively to identify, prioritize, and implement projects. SARP provides technical support to each of these teams on barrier identification, prioritization, and by making additions to the inventory when team members identify undocumented barriers.

## Arkansas Climate Resiliency

Many factors impacting the Southeast regional landscape are often too pervasive for any one agency to address independently, like sea-level rise, changing weather patterns, and habitat loss.
While Arkansas must evaluate the impact of landscape level stressors and threats like climate change and land use change within its own boundaries, management efforts must also be coordinated on a regional scale. The AFWA 2022 (2nd Edition) Voluntary Guidance for States to Incorporate Climate Adaptation into State Wildlife Action Plans provides recommended steps for developing and implementing adaptation strategies. Much of the guidance includes taking a broader, regional approach to incorporating climate adaptation into SWAPs.
Projections of Earth's future surface temperatures depend primarily on future emissions and how sensitive the climate system will be to these emissions. Projections of climate change can be examined using two approaches: 1) global warming levels (GWLs); and 2) scenarios.
• GWLs are defined levels of warming for Earth's average temperature that correspond to increases of 2.7 °F (1.5 °C), 3.6 °F (2 °C), 5.4 °F (3 °C), and 7.2 °F (4 °C) degrees above preindustrial levels 
(IPCC, 2018;
Arias et al., 2021)
.
• A scenario-based approach can be used to explore plausible climate outcomes under a coherent future trajectory of greenhouse gases and other anthropogenic forcings 
(Leung et al., 2023)
.
The Fourth National Climate Assessment (NCA4), used projections created with Representative Concentration Pathways (RCPs) to project and talk about future climate in the United States 
(USGCRP, 2018)
. However, the Fifth National Climate Assessment (NCA5) used newly developed climate projections created with Shared Socioeconomic Pathways (SSPs), that represent plausible trajectories of GDP and population growth, and trends of economic and technological progress globally for the 21 st -century 
(USGCRP, 2023)
. The climate projections used in the NCA5
were created using global climate models from the Coupled Model Intercomparison Project Phase 6 (CMIP6) that were statistically downscaled to translate large scale changes in the climate under a given SSP or GWL to local scales for impact assessment and planning 
(Basile et al., 2023)
.
Three of the SSPs used with the new climate models from the CMIP6 correspond to three of the RCPs used with past global climate models from the Coupled Model Intercomparison Project Phase 5 (CMIP5) in their overall forcing levels (SSP1-2.6 corresponds with RCP2.6, SSP2-4.5 with RCP4.5, and SSP5-8.5 with RCP8.5) 
(Leung et al., 2023)
. Using scenarios can help inform when the planet will reach a certain global warming level and throughout this section, a mix of the SSPs and GWLs data will be used to show climate impacts.
Figure 
5
.8. This figure represents an example of how various scenarios can be linked to specific GWLs and different timescales and is taken from the NCA5, Figure 
2
.14 
(Marvel et al. 2023)
. In this example, the IPCC AR6 assessed warming projections for four future scenarios, with projected years at which the 2°C (3.6°F) global warming level would be reached. For example, under a very high scenario (SSP5-8.5), models project reaching 2°C between 2033 and 2054, with an average estimate of 2042. Under a low scenario (SSP1-2.6), the 5% CI (confidence interval) range begins in 2041, but the average projection shows that warming would actually stay below 2°C.
Using available climate change data and information that assumes a specific Global Warming Level (GWL) or SSP-based scenario can allow land managers to consider possible future conditions. Exactly when and if we reach a particular GWL depends on the trends of Global emissions and how sensitive the climate system is to these emissions (Figure 
5
.8).
• For example, in a very high scenario such as SSP5-8.5, the Earth is very likely to exceed a GWL of 2 (3.6 °F) between 2033 and 2054, depending on the climate sensitivity to greenhouse gas emissions from each global climate model 
(Marvel et al., 2023;
Figure 5.8
).
• By contrast, in a low scenario such as SSP1-2.6, the Earth is very likely not to cross GWL 2 at all 
(Marvel et al., 2023;
Figure 5.8)
.
However, the rate of global warming varies across these scenarios, as well, with high and very high scenarios assuming the Earth warms faster 
(Marvel et al., 2023)
. Since 1850, the Earth's temperature has risen by an average of 0.11° Fahrenheit per decade and, as of 2023, rising about 2 °F in total (Climate Change: Global Temperature | NOAA Climate.gov). However, since 1982, the rate of warming increased over 3 times as fast to about 0.36°F per decade. Currently, the planet is estimated to warm by nearly 5.4°F by the end of this century, meaning crossing a GWL 3 threshold, despite current global emission reduction pledges, according to the most recent United Nation's Annual 2023 Emissions Gap Report (Emissions Gap Report 2023 | UNEP -UN Environment Programme). Based on these trends, it is likely that the habitats, ecosystems, and conditions of Virginia will also change as faster rates of climate change also increase the challenge of adaptation for natural systems and wildlife.
Climate projections use the best available physical science to predict how the climate system will respond to changes in human activities, but not all climate projections are developed in the same manner 
(IPCC, 2023)
. Climate experts across the world have produced more than 50 global climate models under multiple greenhouse gas emissions and socioeconomic scenarios, each with unique variations and nuances. Some have undergone transformations like downscaling to improve spatial resolutions for smaller areas in order to make management decisions at a local scale.
The most recent climate projections used in the NCA5 and associated climate parameters can be viewed on the National Climate Change Viewer. This tool, developed by the U.S. Geological

## Arkansas and Climate Hazards

Due to Arkansas's position in the interior southern US, close but not adjacent to the Gulf, its resulting climate has moderately large variations in temperatures and abundant precipitation 
(Runkle et al. 2022)
. The annual climate is characterized by hot, humid summers, while winters are typically short and cool with episodic blasts of cold arctic air 
(Runkle et al. 2022)
. The northwestern quadrant of Arkansas that includes the Ozark Mountains, and particularly the Boston Mountains portion of the range, is generally higher in elevation than the rest of the state and thus usually experiences cooler temperatures 
(Runkle et al. 2022)
. Precipitation is abundant throughout the year, with winter and spring being the wettest seasons, and heavy rains can produce totals of over 10 inches 
(Runkle et al. 2022)
.
Like the rest of the Southeast, Arkansas is predicted to experience a number of climate impacts, though the magnitude of change often varies by ecoregion, emissions scenario, and future timeframe. Generally, these impacts include hotter summers, warmer winters, droughts and more frequent extreme weather events 
(USGCRP 2018
(USGCRP , 2023))
.
Hotter Days, Nights and Warmer Winters Temperatures in Arkansas have risen by 0.5°F since the beginning of the 20 th century, which is about less than a third of the average warming for the contiguous U.S. 
(Runkle et al. 2022)
.
However, the warmest consecutive 5-year interval for the state was 2015-2019 
(Runkle et al. 2022)
. Additionally, heatwaves in the U.S. have become hotter, more frequent, larger, and last longer lasting in recent decades. This trend is projected to continue, which has implications for drought in the region 
(Hoffman, 2023)
. Since the mid 1950's the annual number of extremely hot days (i.e., maximum daytime temperature of 100°F or higher) has generally been below average for Arkansas; presumably due to increased cloud cover and precipitation and other factors 
(Runkle et al. 2022)
. Overall, the models summarized in the Fifth National Climate Assessment project that the number of extremely hot days (maximum temperatures ≥100°F) will increase across Arkansas, with the most pronounced increases occurring in the west gulf coastal plain ecoregion, though the magnitude of change often varies greatly by global warming level and location within the state (Table 
5
.5; Figure 
5
.9). 
(Williams et al., 2014)
. Shorter and warmer winters may cause species range shifts that would allow for warm-adapted species to dominate and shift distributions of cold-adapted species.
Overall, the models summarized in the Fifth National Climate Assessment project that the number of extremely cold days (i.e., days with low temperatures of ≤ 32°F) in Arkansas will of annual 3-inch extreme precipitation events: the average for the 2015-2020 period averaged 1.8 days per year while the long-term average is 1.1 days per year 
(Runkle et al. 2022)
.
Table 
3
 summarizes approximate values for historical observed and future projections of extreme rainfall under different SSP scenarios for Arkansas Ecoregions; while Figure 
4
 Arkansas also regularly experiences tornados and between 1985-2020, the state has averaged approximately 32 tornadoes per year 
(Runkle et al. 2022)
. Climate change will likely make extreme weather events, like tornados, more common but predicting whether climate change will have a direct effect on the frequency and power of tornados is a challenge. For all their destructive fury, tornadoes are short-lived, lasting from a few seconds to hours as opposed to days or weeks at time and span a much shorter area than hurricanes -this makes them very difficult to model in climate simulations to project the effects of climate change. Instead, we can examine how climate change might affect the individual weather "ingredients" that support the development of supercell thunderstorms that produce tornadoes which include warm, moist air, an unstable atmosphere, and wind shear (i.e., wind at different levels moving in different directions at different speeds). While there have been no long-term trends in the frequency of tornados, there have been changes in tornado patterns. Recent tornado research has been
showing evidence for two phenomenon: 1) tornado events are becoming more clustered (i.e., there are fewer days with at least one tornado per year but more days with over 30) 
(Tippett et al. 2016)
, and 2) tornado patterns have shifted geographically 
(Gensini & Brooks 2018)
. The number of tornadoes in the states that used to make up "Tornado Alley" are falling, while tornado events have been on the rise in Arkansas and its bordering states 
(Gensini & Brooks 2018)
. Wildlife are most impacted by tornadoes via wind damage to their habitats, for example, the removal of forest canopy. Species composition of breeding bird populations in the Arkansas Ozarks have been found to differ between forests of varying tornado damage: with heavily damaged forests exhibiting significantly less abundance of typical forest species (e.g., red-eyed vireo, ovenbird) than undamaged forests, and with undamaged forests containing higher abundance of edge species (e.g., indigo bunting, white-eyed vireo) 
(Smith et al. 2004
).
Although little change in total annual precipitation is projected over this century for the 

## 2022).

The Southeast has the highest aquatic diversity of any temperate system; however, the ecological relationships and life histories of many of the endemic species are not yet well understood within the constraints of climate change 
(Ingram et al., 2013)
. Freshwater ecosystems (e.g., streams, rivers, lakes, and wetlands) of the Southeast are highly vulnerable to warming. Impacts on rare species of fish and mussels are of particular concern in a changing climate. The combined effects of warming and changes in precipitation will likely alter overall hydrology including increased evapotranspiration and reduced stream base flow 
(Ingram et al., 2013)
 Currently, many aquatic species declines are associated with widespread alteration of flow regimes and are expected to be exacerbated by climate change. Impacts of climate change on riverine and wetland ecosystems are predicted to also include increased water temperatures, which dictate many species ranges and exacerbates low dissolved oxygen conditions 
(Ingram et al., 2013)
. In general, temperature regimes of freshwater ecosystems are projected to increase in parallel with shifts in air temperatures, which in turn may alter plant and animal communities, including migratory birds 
(Princé and Zuckerberg 2015)
.

## Measuring Species' Response to Climate Change

As the climate, regional landscape, and weather patterns change, there is value in identifying regional strategies and conservation actions to support the SGCN and RSGCN that are most vulnerable to the impacts. A species is considered vulnerable to climate change if it is both sensitive and exposed to climate change impacts 
(Williams et al., 2008;
Figure 5.13)
. For example, a species may be exposed to climate change because it experiences an impact like warmer stream temperatures, but if it is resilient to that change or able to disperse into new habitats, it may ultimately have low vulnerability. Conversely, a species that would be sensitive to climate change but is unlikely to experience any impacts and is therefore not exposed, may also not be understanding if a species is sensitive and will be exposed to climate change can help managers develop strategies to support the species most likely to be impacted. Ecological niche models (also called species distribution models) start by identifying shared climate conditions at sites where a species is known to have occurred and then predict where similar climate conditions may be found on the landscape in the future. Some models also take into account the ability of a species to disperse to reach any new areas that may become climatically suitable. The resulting predictions from these models allow researchers to create spatially explicit estimates of whether suitable climate conditions for a species are likely to remain stable, become more widespread in the landscape (i.e. increase), or become more geographically rare in the future (i.e. decrease). For example, if suitable climate conditions for a species were projected to decrease greatly in an area in the future, then the species would be considered more vulnerable to climate change than a species for which climate conditions were predicted to remain stable. It is important to note that raw data from niche models (e.g., spatial raster data) is not usually readily available to the public due to the large size of the files but instead is available in summary form in the research literature (i.e., academic journal articles).
Therefore, more translational effort is needed with niche model results as the form of the data might not be readily interpretable by users.
Niche model results can be used in tandem with assessments to enhance the understanding of a given species' vulnerability, because assessments and niche models have complementary 

## Comparing CCVI and Available Ecological Niche Models Results

Comparing the results of the ecological niche models to the CCVI estimates for SGCNs within Arkansas reveals similar trends for taxa that were evaluated using both methods. All taxa (including plants) assessed to date will experience some vulnerability to climate change (i.e., moderately, highly, extremely vulnerable) and some decreases in suitable range due to climate change (i.e., decrease or large decrease). Looking across all the taxa besides birds, however, these results suggest that amphibians, fish, insects and mussels are projected to have large proportions of Arkansas SGCN vulnerable to climate change. Amphibians have the fewest species with predicted stable populations across both methods and show virtually no SGCN to be presumed stable or increase in the CCVI estimates. Birds have the most species identified as vulnerable to climate change to date, but also the greatest most species with predicted stable or increasing populations across both approaches, partially due to how many more bird SGCN have been evaluated compared to other taxa.
