* Wintertime precipitation is especially projected to increase in Arkansas by midcentury, with the increase being in the form of rain rather than snow(Runkle et al 2022). In the other seasons, precipitation changes are uncertain. However, increases in evaporation rates due to rising temperatures may increase the rate of soil moisture loss during dry spells, and as a result, naturally occurring droughts are projected to be more intense in the state(Runkle et al. 2022; Stevens et al. 2023). Future projected changes in the annual number of consecutive extreme dry days in Arkansas are only projected to increase by 4-6% by 2050-2074 (Alder 2024). However, higher temperatures throughout the year in Arkansas will increase the rate of soil moisture loss (i.e., evapotranspiration) during dry spells, which could lead to more intense droughts(Runkle et al.*

## Prairies and Savannas

Lower Mississippi Alluvial Plain Grand Prairie and Savanna Ozark-Ouachita Prairie and Savanna West Gulf Coastal Plain Calcareous Prairie and Savanna
Survey, allows for visualizations of climate change impacts, such as days of extreme heat under projects of degree warming days, under SSP-based scenarios and Global Warming Levels 
(Alder and Hostetler, 2013)
. Additionally, these data are available as interactive maps and layers as part of the Fifth National Climate Assessment Interactive Atlas (National Climate Assessment
Interactive Atlas) and is where we have derived our spatial climate data for this report.
Information pertaining to sea-level rise, as well as easy-to-understand state-level summaries on climate change impacts, are available from the National Oceanic and Atmospheric Administration (NOAA) via their Sea-level Rise Technical Reports and Sea-level Rise Viewer Tool, and their State Climate Summaries website 
(Runkle and Kunkel et al., 2022;
Sweet et al., 2022)
.
In the Southeast, climate change will have pervasive impacts on natural habitats, ecological processes, and ecosystem services and regional efforts to mitigate or adapt to these impacts will be extremely important. According to NCA4 and NCA5, conditions in the Southeast may change dramatically 
(USGCRP, 2018
(USGCRP, & 2023))
. Both Assessments indicate that current trends will continue such as increasing frequency, intensity, and duration of heatwaves, increasing wildfire risk, and rising sea levels.
The most pertinent projected changes include extreme heat, extreme precipitation events, drought, sea-level rise, and tropical cyclones, as well as decreases in the intensity and frequency of cold-season events. Despite the significant threat climate change poses to the landscape, understanding the tools and resources available to address climate change and develop effective strategies is difficult. As part of the survey administered to SWAP coordinators in 2021, the majority of SEAFWA states/territories reported integrating climate change as one of the most challenging aspects of the upcoming SWAP revision 
(SECAS, 2021)
. Anecdotally, some SWAP coordinators noted that it is difficult to discern what tools are available, decide which tool is the right one to use, and choose the appropriate scale and timeframe when using tools. Some reported it is also challenging to determine what climate-change associated threats to include and how to spatially represent their impacts.
decrease between 14-21 fewer days under GWL 2 across the vast majority of the state, with the exception of Chicot County, though the magnitude of change often varies by location within the state (Table 
5
.6; Figure 
5
.10). strengths that can help land managers make informed conservation decisions. Niche models offer more refined and spatially explicit predictions (i.e., changes in suitable habitat) about the direct exposure of a species to climate change but are only available for a limited set of species, in part because the models require extensive data on a species before they can be applied.
Assessments use an expert judgment-based approach that allows integration of different types most of which to date have focused on birds 
(Armsworth et al., 2025a
(Armsworth et al., & 2025b))
. Many of these CCVI assessments indicate that while some bird species may be vulnerable, a larger proportion may remain stable or increase under changing climatic conditions. Most CCVI assessments done on Arkansas's SGCN to date have focused on birds 
(Armsworth et al. 2025a
). Many of these CCVI assessments indicate that while some bird species may be vulnerable, a larger proportion may remain stable or increase under changing climatic conditions. Though more assessments are needed for other taxa, these current CCVI estimates show that some Arkansas SGCN across all taxa are vulnerable to climate change: with amphibians, fish, invertebrates, plants and reptiles having the highest proportions. For example, all of the assessed amphibian and plant SGCN in Arkansas to date are vulnerable to climate change (Figure 
5
.14).
However, there are currently few overlaps between the species assessed by both the CCVI and ecological niche model datasets (limiting the scope of comparisons until more assessments and niche models are done). In particular, niche model estimates are not yet available for many aquatic species such as fishes and mussels, which the scientific literature suggests may be some of the most vulnerable to climate change in Arkansas and the Southeast Region 
(Halpern and Kappel 2012;
Poff et al. 2012;
Ingram et al. 2013)
. However, the available CCVI for these aquatic taxa suggest that fish and mussels have the greatest numbers of Arkansas SGCN predicted to be vulnerable to climate change than not (Figure 
5
.14).
The comparison of these two methods supports focusing climate adaptation strategies on the conservation of amphibians and aquatic species. SARP already considers identification and remediation of anthropogenic barriers to aquatic organism passage a priority conservation action, and this can also be an important climate adaptation strategy to support the resilience of fish and other species that rely on aquatic connectivity.

## Adaptation Strategies

Identifying adaptation strategies to address impacts and vulnerability is a step towards climate resiliency. Climate adaptation and mitigation comes in various forms, and multiple frameworks have been built to support planning for and implementing climate adaptation. Most climate adaptation frameworks generally include:
• Recognizing limitations to adaptation, such as the degree of natural resource management, availability of funding, or staffing capacity; and
• Deciding which type(s) of adaptation approach(es) meet specific conservation goals. Appendix A: Species Profiles Species profiles have been developed for all 520 SGCN. These species profiles are meant to serve as a quick reference guide to identify an SGCN's priority tier, conservation status, general distribution within the state, habitat associations, threats, conservation actions, data gaps, and some other basic information.
The profiles are organized below by taxa group: Mammals, Birds, Reptiles, Amphibians, Fish, Crayfish, Mussels, Insects, Other Invertebrates, Plants.

## Desired Future Conditions

Desired future conditions have been well published, broadly for some species of greatest conservation need and ecoregions, but are rarely defined for specific habitat types at the stand level. We used published DFCs when available as a starting point, refined by expertise of land managers from several different agencies and professional backgrounds. DFCs published here are meant to serve as a guide for land managers to aid in identifying, restoring and managing habitat types to mimic their pre-settlement condition for maximum species (plant and animal) diversity. These values are meant to be a steady state goal, after restoration work is completed. In practice, a property level (a 5,000 ac. WMA for example) goal may be for all stands of the same habitat type to average to the stated DFC. Many stands will receive treatments outside of DFCs to project a trajectory toward DFCs in the future. For example, an upland hardwood forest that has experienced densification may have canopy closure levels and basal area above DFC levels, resulting in below DFC understory herbaceous coverage. That stand would be treated to below DFC canopy closure and basal areas to begin a trajectory toward DFCs after 20 years of management and growth. Also included in the DFC section are common pitfalls that should be mitigated by managers. Dominant species are those that should dominate through abundance and biomass, when at desired condition in a steady state. Indicator species are conspicuous examples that help managers delineate habitat types from other, similar types. These species may or may not be desirable when at desired condition or dominant in abundance or biomass. For example, mature Eastern red cedar is often an indicator of a degraded glade, even though it is not desirable in a restored example. Understory herbaceous indicator species are often the last holdouts in degraded habitats. For example, rattlesnake master often persists in remnant prairie after decades of woody intrusion and without fire.
Likewise, fire and flooding parameters are given as ideals for steady state examples, though desired regimes may diverge temporarily for management reasons, especially during the restoration phase. For example, fires in glades may be restricted to dormant season when fuel loading is high in the restoration phase, despite the stated DFC for fire being in the late growing season. In bottomland hardwood habitats, flooding may be avoided after underplanting oaks and dormant season flooding resumed after seedlings mature.

## Landscape Level Habitat Name

Crop Land

## Habitat Description

This type includes cultivated fields or aquaculture ponds, often many acres in size, managed specifically for a single crop. Natural and historic habitat type can vary from prairie to forest. In Arkansas, crop lands in riparian areas and flood plains are a high priority for conservation in programs such as Conservation Reserve Program, Wetland Reserve Easements and others. These programs convert crop land to prairie, herbaceous wetland or bottomland hardwood functional facsimiles.
Natureserve Crosswalk N/A AWAP Land Class 70 Guild Cropland Secondary Guild Habitat Team (database only) Nick Goforth (AGFC), Steve Burgess (AGFC), Allison Fowler (AGFC), Ben Benton (ANHC), Clint Johnson (AGFC), Doug Zollner (TNC), Emily Roberts (AGFC), Ethan Lum (AGFC), Tucker Collins (AGFC), Jessica Novobilsky (ANHC), Joshua Harris (AGFC), Lee Chambers (AGFC), Michael Gregory (AGFC), Randy Brents (AGFC), Rob Willey (AGFC), Theo Witsell (SGI) and Lorne Green (AGFC) SGCN Associations (assigned) Demonstration Areas Spatial Ecology Narrative This habitat occurs statewide in all ecoregions on a variety of historical habitat types. Cropland is heavily modified and typically does not resemble natural vegetation conditions.

## Goal

Landscape connectivity is high. Increasing crop connectivity is not a conservation concern. Habitat Condition Narrative While cropland is highly modified from natural habitat conditions, there are conservation practices that can and should be implemented. Filter strips of native herbaceous vegetation and windbreaks with native trees provide critical ecosystem services, including runoff filtration and provide habitat for some SGCN species. Examples include grassland birds and insects and water birds. There is much work to do in injecting conservation measures into agricultural practices, where possible, including native grass filter strips, post-harvest flooding, delayed soil disturbance, cover crops, etc. USDA programs are of critical importance in this effort.

## Goal

Work with agriculture industry on injecting conservation measures into agriculture.

## Crop Land

Stand Level Desired Conditions Narrative (restoration and maintenance) Conservation work on row crop hinges upon developing incorporating practices that benefit the environment while benefiting, or at least being neutral to agricultural operations. Successful practices typically take advantage of less productive times or portions of fields to inject conservation benefits.

## Conservation action (Restoration)

In some cases, low productivity fields or portions of fields can be removed from production and placed in conservation use to improve overall agricultural operations. Examples include USDA programs to remove frequently flooded fields or field edges from production.

## Landscape Level Habitat Name

Crowley's Ridge Sand Forest Habitat Description
This system of upland Short-Leaf Pine -hardwood forests is confined to Crowley's Ridge on the western side of the Mississippi River. This vegetation is very distinctive from that of the adjacent alluvial plain and the ridge itself also contrasts sharply with the adjacent alluvial plain. Crowley's Ridge is a remnant loess-capped feature rising from 30 m to over 60 m (100-200 feet) above the alluvial plain surface, to about 150 m (450 feet) above sea level.
The base of the northern ridge is composed of Tertiary substrates overlain by alluvial deposits and capped with generally thin layers of Pleistocene loess. The Pleistocene alluvial deposits are often sandy and in a very limited area, there are outcrops of sandstone of uncertain origin. 

## Narrative

This habitat occurs statewide in all ecoregions on a variety of historical habitat types. While plantations are heavily modified from historical habitat conditions, cultivated forests can serve as useable habitat for many SGCN, when they are managed to replicate open pine woodland conditions. In the Gulf Coastal Plain, pine plantation has become the matrix habitat type and landscape connectivity can only be achieved for many species by managing plantations as facsimiles of natural pine woodlands. In the Ouachitas and Ozarks, plantations may be the only habitat types on the landscape displaying grassland-like vegetation. In the Mississippi Alluvial Plain, plantations may be the only existing forested habitats that do not flood.

## Goal

Increase landscape woodland connectivity through fee title acquisition, conservation easements, partnering with industrial owners and technical assistance and funding with non-industrial owners.

## Habitat Condition

## Narrative

The vast majority of pine plantations are managed for timber production only, though they can be thinned and burned to balance timber production and wildlife habitat, with little impact to revenue. Management can also be tailored speciifcally toward wildlife habitat, if production is not a goal. While plant conservation may not be attainable, plantations can serve as habitat for many SGCN, if managed properly.

## Goal

Work with industrial and-non industrial owners to balance production and 

## Narrative

This habitat type historically occurred in large tracts of relatively flat, unbroken terrain which were maintained by frequent fire. Though in the Mississippi Alluvial Plain, these areas lie well above the active floodplain. Prior to European settlement, these prairies covered about 400,000 ac. in a matrix of savanna and woodland covering another 500,000 ac. Soils were highly productive for agriculture and easily converted, with less than 1,000 ac. left in high quality remanants today and another couple thousand acres of recreations. Connectivity and edge effects from herbicide overspray are signifigant issues in these remanining examples. Almost all remaining examples are in conservation ownership, with modern efforts focused on recreations and facsimiles to increase connectivity.

## Goal

Seek opportunities on public and private land to increase connectivity through recreations and facsimiles. Habitat Condition

## Narrative

Remaining examples, though in conservation ownership, are small and isolated enough to be threatened by edge effects from surrounding agricultural land uses, including woody encroachment, difficulty in safely applying prescribed fire, siltation, altered hydrology and herbicide overspray. Management of remanants must be done very carefully to avoid damage, particularly in herbicide use on encroahcing trees and timing of prescribed fire to replicate natural fire regimes. The greatest opportunity is to increase connectivity through recreations and functional facsimiles on surrounding private lands through USDA conservation programs, as the vast majority of historic prairie acreage is in private ownership. Opportunities to create functional examples also exist on conservation lands currently managed for wetlands, as most acres in public ownership lie lower on the landscape than prairies historically occured. Most conservation ownership today is on land too low and too wet to farm.

## Goal

Continue to maintain remaning examples for maximum species diversity and seek opportunities on public and private land to increase connectivity through recreations and facsimiles. 

## Narrative

This habitat type historically occured within the annual floodplain of the Mississippi River and its tributaries, specifically in depressions and backswamps, often along ancient river channels. The major disturbance is catostrophic and long duration floods, as well as wind events during periods of saturated soils that topple large trees. Fire would have occured at higher elevations, but only backing into these habitats in severe droughts. Many of the historical examples still remain intact, although altered hydrology (both decreasing and increasing flooding) has been a significant impact. Other examples have been converted to agriculture through drainage and channelization, while other examples have been lost to impoundments. In geologic time, channelization of rivers that no longer meander may interrupt the life cycle of river meanders being cut off to become new bottomland depressions. Since these habitats are too wet for agriculture, many examples exist intact both on private lands and in conservation ownership. These examples are often the last remaining natural habitats within a heavily modified landscape.

## Goal

Maintain natural hydrologic regimes. Habitat Condition Narrative These habitats typically exist with intact forest communities, though with often altered hydrology. When located further from a large river, these examples are often removed from the floodplain by levees, resulting in lower depth and frequency of flooding. When located closer to a large river and inside of levees, these habitats may be altered by increasing flooding frequency and depth (particularly into the growing season), which may be excacerbated by beaver activity 

## Narrative

This habitat type historically occurred along the edges of large tracts of relatively flat, unbroken terrain which were maintained by frequent fire (Grand Prairie) and were maintained in a relatevely open condition by fires occasional backing downhill and eventually self extinguishing. Though in the Mississippi Alluvial Plain, these areas lie well above the active floodplain. These woodlands existed along a spectrum from savanna to closed canopy forest, as fire freqency and severity decreased toward streams. Many of these acres were cleared for agriculture in the 20th This system is composed of riverfront associations, generally temporarily (but rarely seasonally) flooded on point bars and natural levees adjacent to the river that formed them, with flooding more frequent than every 5 years, by flowing water directly from the stream. They occur along the lower Mississippi River and its tributaries. 

## Narrative

This habitat type historically occured in the Mississippi Alluvial Plain directly adjacent to major rivers, inside the annual floodplain. Thousands of acres remain in good shape and connected to the floodplain in conservation ownership. These conserved lands are often intertwined across multiple agency ownerships to form large (over 100,000 ac.), contiguous tracts. The greatest threat is altered hydrology, with channelized rivers and levee systems extending flooding duration and depth in these habitats.

## Goal

Continue to increase connectivity through easements and fee title acquisition. 

## Narrative

Examples of this habitat type, by definition, are islands, only occuring on the flat tops of the tallest peaks in the Ouachitas and Arkansas River Valley, above 2,600 ft. elevation. All of these examples are in conservation ownership, though they are at risk from recreational use (soil compaction, erosion, invasive species and increased wildfire risk). Climate change will likely imapct the elevations where these unique habitats occur over time. Another threat is the unknown ecology of these systems, as they are poorly understood.

## Goal

Protect the sites where this habitat type exists.

## Habitat Condition

The relative condition of this habitat type across the landscape and its suitability to support SGCN.

## Narrative

Due to the rare and unique nature of these habitats, desired conditions are not well understood, so condition is not well known.

## Goal

Unknown.
Ouachita 

## Narrative

This habitat type historically occured as pockets with varying degrees of connectivity, dictated by geology. Glades can on ridgetops, above cliffs, on prominent points, along streams and other locations where bedrock may be exposed. These were typically small, often linear openings in a matrix of open woodland, though some examples of large (over 1,000 ac.) glade complexes do exist. Connectivity for flora and fauna existed through these more or less contiguous grasslands (parts forested, parts not), with forest overstory varying with soil type. Plant and presumably animal communities can vary over small geographic areas, reacting to changes in soil chemistry. Glade flora and fauna are a mix of tallgrass prairie species that occur in the surrounding woodland matrix and glade specialists species. Of the latter, many are either endemics that may occupy small geographic areas (some restricted to a single soil type in one county in Arkansas) or are a small, disjunct population separated by hundreds of miles. These disjunct populations were "captured" as semi-arid flora and fauna during dry climactic periods in recent geologic history and the majority of these species' ranges occur south and west of Arkansas, many as far as the Sonoran desert.

## Goal

Protect high-quality remnants via fee-title acquisitions or easements, focusing on increasing connectivity and on expanding or connecting protected areas. These projects must include restoration of the woodland matrix around glades, in addition to the glades themselves. Location of remnants have been well mapped, but the quality of many of these and their restoration potential are unknown. Habitat Condition Narrative Today, the majority of historic acreage has been lost or degraded due to fire suppression and land use changes (i.e., urbanization, road construction, rock quarrying, livestock grazing, silviculture, etc.). Glades were often the first locations disturbed at time of settlement, because they were the only places devoid of trees. The remaining fragments of this habitat type are scattered across the landscape and vary in size and condition. Consequently, the protection and restoration of these areas remains a priority to help conserve the many glade-dependent species that rely on them. Remaining examples that were encroached by cedar or other trees with few other impacts generally have intact seedbanks and can be restored.
Conversely, examples that have been severely demaged by grazing and rock quarrying may lack intact seedbanks and restoration potential. Recreations and facsimiles are typically not possible, as the specialized plants that occur in glades are currently not availalbe commercially. This fact has emphasized the importance of properly maintaining remaining examples and identifying and restoring other remnants. While much of this habitat type has already been lost or degraded and some threats still remain, much work has and can still be done to balance production and habitat quality on working lands, as well as continuing to convey the importance of protecting and conserving these areas. Protected areas should be managed to promote biodiversity and resiliency and to control invasive species and woody encroachment by restoring the historic fire regime and with mechanical and chemical treatment methods, when necessary. Special care should be taken to preserve the remaining biodiversity by using targeted and/or limited herbicide applications and mitigating fire severity with fuel reduction burns. Management of restored sites should also be done with care, as improper fire timing can harm specialized plant species, many of which are under studied. Restore and manage protected areas to increase the quality of remaining This system occurs along moderate to steep slopes or valley walls of rivers along most aspects. Parent material includes chert, shale and/or sandstone bedrock with well-to excessively well-drained, shallow soils interspersed with rock and boulders. These soils are typically dry during the summer and autumn, becoming saturated during the spring and winter. Grasses such as Little Bluestem and Indian Grass dominate this system with stunted oak species Post Oak, Blackjack Oak and shrub species such as Vaccinium spp. occurring on variable depth soils. This system is influenced by drought and infrequent to occasional fires. Prescribed fires help manage this system by maintaining an open glade structure. These systems are usually small, isolated and/or disjunct and are often "embedded" in a larger habitat matrix. These systems rely heavily on surrounding and/or adjacent habitats for landscape scale functions and processes such as fire. 

## Narrative

This habitat type historically occured as pockets with varying degrees of connectivity, dictated by geology. Glades can on ridgetops, above cliffs, on prominent points, along streams and other locations whre bedrock may be exposed. These were typically small, often linear openings in a matrix of open woodland, though some examples of large (over 1,000 ac.) glade complexes do exist. Connectivity for flora and fauna existed through these more or less contiguous grasslands (parts forested, parts not), with forest overstory varying with soil type. Plant and presumably animal communities can vary over small geographic areas, reacting to changes in soil chemistry. Glade flora and fauna are a mix of tallgrass prairie species that occur in the surrounding woodland matrix and glade specialists species. Of the latter, many are either endemics that may occupy small geographic areas (some restricted to a single soil type in one county in Arkansas) or are a small, disjunct population separated by hundreds of miles. These disjunct populations were "captured" as semi-arid flora and fauna during dry climactic periods in recent geologic history and the majority of these species' ranges occur south and west of Arkansas, many as far as the Sonoran desert.

## Goal

Protect high-quality remnants via fee-title acquisitions or easements, focusing on increasing connectivity and on expanding or connecting protected areas. These projects must include restoration of the woodland matrix around glades, in addition to the glades themselves. Location of remnants have been well mapped, but the quality of many of these and their restoration potential are unknown. Habitat Condition Narrative Today, the majority of historic acreage has been lost or degraded due to fire suppression and land use changes (i.e., urbanization, road construction, rock quarrying, livestock grazing, silviculture, etc.). Glades were often the first locations disturbed at time of settlement, because they were the only places devoid of trees. The remaining fragments of this habitat type are scattered across the landscape and vary in size and condition. Consequently, the protection and restoration of these areas remains a priority to help conserve the many glade-dependent species that rely on them. Remaining examples that were encroached by cedar or other trees with few other impacts generally have intact seedbanks and can be restored.
Conversely, examples that have been severely demaged by grazing and rock quarrying may lack intact seedbanks and restoration potential. Recreations and facsimiles are typically not possible, as the specialized plants that occur in glades are currently not availalbe commercially. This fact has emphasized the importance of properly maintaining remaining examples and identifying and restoring other remnants. While much of this habitat type has already been lost or degraded and some threats still remain, much work has and can still be done to balance production and habitat quality on working lands, as well as continuing to convey the importance of protecting and conserving these areas. Protected areas should be managed to promote biodiversity and resiliency and to control invasive species and woody encroachment by restoring the historic fire regime and with mechanical and chemical treatment methods, when necessary. Special care should be taken to preserve the remaining biodiversity by using targeted and/or limited herbicide applications and mitigating fire severity with fuel reduction burns. Management of restored sites should also be done with care, as improper fire timing can harm specialized plant species, many of which are under studied. Restore and manage protected areas to increase the quality of remaining This habitat type historically occurred in large tracts of relatively flat, unbroken terrain which were maintained by frequent fire. Examples of this are valley floors in the Arkansas River Valley and Ozarks and plateau tops in the Ozarks and Ouachitas with smaller areas occurring on rocky ridges in the Ozarks and Ouachitas. Today, the majority of historic acreage has been lost or degraded due to fire suppression and land use changes (i.e., urbanization, agriculture, livestock grazing, silviculture, etc.). Across the southeast, over 90% of native grasslands have been lost since European colonization. The remaining fragments of this habitat type are scattered across the landscape and vary in

This habitat type is also distinct from Crowley's Ridge Mesic Loess Slope Forest that occur on the nearby slopes. These two habitat types are very visually end ecologically different, but occur intermixed and are dependent on fine scale hydrologic, aspect and soils variations. This type occurs on a spectrum from forest to savanna, depending on fine scale aspect and soil conditions.*

Foti pers. comm.). These dunes are near Crowley's Ridge and the Black and White rivers, above the normal flood level of the Mississippi. Examples in Missouri occur amidst a series of low-lying, anastomosing channels that have helped to protect them from extensive alteration more typical in Arkansas where the uplands have been largely cleared. The uppermost portions of the dunes support a xeric community similar to sandhills of the South Central Plains, but are outside the natural range of some species in that ecoregion. Instead the dunes support very open Post Oak woodlands with Little Bluestem and abundant lichen cover (presumably Cladonia spp.Other historic examples exist in conservation ownership today (Sheffield Nelson Dagmar WMA and Cache River NWR) that were converted to agriculture before coming into conservation ownership. These examples could be managed as functional facsimiles, but the historic seedbanks are long gone.This system is composed of forests, prairies and woodlands on Pleistocene terraces in the Mississippi Alluvial Plain ecoregion. It occurs primarily west of Crowley's Ridge on Pleistocene glacial outwash deposits in Arkansas and Missouri and on Macon Ridge in Louisiana and Arkansas. The sites are above modern floodplains, but have poor internal drainage and are flat with poor runoff, leading to very wet conditions in winter and spring. They also often have a claypan that restricts both internal drainage and, later in the year, water availability. Therefore they are very wet in the winter/spring and very dry in the summer, a moisture regime termed hydroxeric. Because of this moisture regime, the communities are variable, ranging from willow oak flats to post oak flats to prairies. In the 1940s, the Arkansas Game and Fish Commission produced a wildlife habitat map of Arkansas in which these sites were classified as "terrace hardwood forests". These communities have a large variety of upland and lowland tree species, ranging from post oak to overcup oak in a small area. Such species diversity may be explained by regeneration of species with dramatically different moisture tolerances on the same site in dry and wet years on these hydroxeric sites. Because the sites are above current floodplains and susceptible to being drained, they have been cleared at an even greater rate than nearby floodplain forests.*

Work with private landowners on restoring and managing examples on private land.High Bottomlands are often temporarily flooded on older Holocene point bars and natural levees, with flooding less frequent than every 5 years. Wetland functions are primarily driven by precipitation and are classed as hardwood flats in a hydrogeomorphic classification. They are flooded less frequently than adjacent riparian floodplains or low floodplains. These floodplains are of particular conservation interest because they have been cleared to a greater extent than riparian or low floodplains because of the reduced flooding of these sites. Also, flood control levees protect many of these sites and with protection from levees almost all sites are cleared. Thus most wetlands remaining in large bottomland areas are riparian or low bottomlands and the species, communities and other characteristics of high bottomlands have been essentially lost. Forests are often dominated by species such as Cherrybark Oak and Swamp Chestnut Oak. Wildlife agency partners generally would like to see this distinction recognized. Flooding is of shorter duration than on adjacent backswamps, where water is impounded behind riverfront natural levees. Flooding is of longer duration than on high bottomlands that are typically temporarily flooded. Soils are typically sandier than those of low bottomlands. River Cane is a common understory in these forests on natural levees and higher point bars and may become dominant after thinning or removal of overstory. Willow and Eastern Cottonwood sandbars may have an open-canopy (woodlandtype) structure. Often on sites with rapid soil deposition and therefore with rapid development of vegetation from low diversity willow and Eastern Cottonwood dominated communities to more diverse communities dominated by Sycamore, Pecan, Sugarberry, Green Ash or Nuttall's Oak.*

* This system is found along moderate to steep slopes and steep valleys on primarily southerly to westerly facing slopes. Limestone, dolomite or shale bedrock typify this system with shallow, moderately to well-drained soils interspersed with rocks. These soils often dry out during the summer and autumn and then become saturated during the winter and spring. Little Bluestem dominates this system and is commonly associated with Big Bluestem, Sideoats Grama and calcium-loving plant species. Stunted woodlands primarily dominated by Chinquapin Oak interspersed with Juniperus virginiana occur on variable-depth-to-bedrock soils. Fire is the primary natural dynamic and prescribed fires help manage this system by restricting woody growth and maintaining the more open glade structure. These systems are usually small, isolated and/or disjunct and are often embedded in a larger habitat matrix. These systems rely heavily on surrounding and/or adjacent habitats for landscape scale functions and processes such as fire.*

* Goalavailable habitat on the landscape. Special efforts should be made to maintain high-quality sites. Also, work with private landowners to restore habitat on private lands and to implement BMPs. was historically widespread on south and west aspects in the Ozarks and Ouachitas, as well as rolling hills in the Arkansas River Valley. This is one of the more intact habitats in Arkansas today on both private lands and in conservation ownership, though most remaining examples exhibit densification and mesophication from fire exclusion, unless they are actively managed. Conversion to pine plantation and pasture is less common, but has occured. In general, these habitats occur on dry, rocky slopes and few have been cleared. Landscape connectivity today is very good in most areas. Many examples in conservation ownership are at the landscape level. well connected examples still exist, habitat quality is low for almost all private land examples and many conserved examples. Densification and mesophication from a lack of fire and, to a lesser degree, high grading, has altered forest structure and composition and severely limited herbaceous plant diversity in the understory. These degraded examples are currently restorable through silvicultural methods and reintroduction of fire, which are well studied and understood. Lack of markets for forest products is a limiting Ozark-Ouachita Dry Oak and Pine Woodland was historically widespread on south and west aspects in the Ozarks and Ouachitas, as well as rolling hills in the Arkansas River Valley. This is one of the more intact habitats in Arkansas today on both private lands and in conservation ownership, though most remaining examples exhibit densification and mesophication from fire exclusion, unless they are actively managed. Conversion to pine plantation and pasture is less common, but has occured. In general, these habitats occur on dry, rocky slopes and few have been cleared. Landscape connectivity today is very good in most areas. Many examples in conservation ownership are at the landscape level. well connected examples still exist, habitat quality is low for almost all private land examples and many conserved examples. Densification and mesophication from a lack of fire and, to a lesser degree, high grading, has altered forest structure and composition and severely limited herbaceous plant diversity in the understory. These degraded examples are currently restorable through silvicultural methods and reintroduction of fire, which are well studied and understood. Lack of markets for forest products is a limiting This system of seeps may be found along the bottom slopes of smaller valleys where rock fractures or sandy soils allow water to seep out of the mountainsides. The soil remains saturated to very moist throughout the year. The vegetation is typically forested with highly variable canopy composition. In acid seeps, vegetation is characterized by Red Maple, Black Gum, Sweetgum and White Oak. Other canopy species may include Beech and Umbrella Magnolia. Canopy coverage can be moderately dense to quite open. The subcanopy is often well-developed and characteristically includes American Holly, Umbrella Magnolia, Musclewood and Hop-hornbeam. Calcareous seeps or fens, typically in the Ozarks, may be dominated by shrubs or herbs such as Large-leaf Grass-of-Parnassus and Carex spp. Many are less than one hectare in area, but riparian seeps are often much larger. These systems are usually small, isolated and/or disjunct and are embedded in a larger habitat matrix. These systems rely heavily on surrounding and/or adjacent habitats for landscape scale functions and processes such as fire. occurs as pockets of forested wetland within an upland forest matrix, generally not connected to other examples. Examples of this system of forested seeps may be found along the bottom slopes of smaller valleys of the Ouachita Mountains of central Arkansas (where rock fractures allow water to seep out of the mountainsides) and in the riparian zones of larger creeks, sometimes extending upslope along small ephemeral drainages. Soils remain satured year round. More information is needed for examples in the Ozarks. Many examples remain on protected lands. Even in developed areas, these seeps are often left undeveloped, though they may be degraded by surrounding land use (livestock, hydrologic alterations, roads, forestry activities, etc.). , for the most part, intact and occur on protected lands. The most siginficant moden threat is impacts due to management on the surrounding uplands. Examples include livestock access on private land and firebreak and timber harvest impacts on both private and public land. BMPs already exist to protect these areas and should be followed. Goal Continue protecting examples with existing BMPs. This floodplain system occurs along larger upland rivers where topography and alluvial processes have resulted in a recognizable floodplain. Many examples of this system will contain well-drained levees, terraces and stabilized bars and some will include herbaceous sloughs and shrub wetlands resulting, in part, from beaver activity. A variety of soil types may be found within the floodplain from very well-drained gravelly substrates to very dense clays. This variety of substrates in combination with different flooding regimes creates a mix of vegetation. Most areas are inundated at some point each spring; microtopography determines how long the various habitats are inundated. Although vegetation is quite variable in this broadly defined system, examples may include Silver Maple, Sycamore, Sweet-gum, River Birch and Quercus spp. Understory species include shrubs, such as Buttonbush and River Cane and Cyperaceae spp. (Carex spp.). This system likely floods at least annually and can be altered by occasional severe floods.Impoundments and conversion to agriculture can also impact this system. An example of this habitat is the floodplain along the Buffalo River. , reforestation of riparian pastures can improve aquatic resources and hydrology, as well as restore this habitat type. On conserved lands, these examples are typically intact and at desired conditions, though impacts can be seen from roads, recreation and forestry land uses. BMPs are well established and should be followed.This system represents forests and woodlands in which Short-leaf Pine is an important or dominant component. Although examples of this system occur throughout this region, there is local variation in the extent to which they were present. For example, this system was historically prominent only in the southeastern part of the Ozark Highlands where sandstone derived soils were common; being limited from other areas by inadequate winter precipitation and non-conducive soils. In contrast, pine was "virtually ubiquitous in the historical forests of the Ouachitas". In nearly all cases (at least in the Ouachitas), Short-leaf Pine occurs with a variable mixture of hardwood species. The exact composition of the hardwoods is much more closely related to aspect and topographic factors than is the pine component. In some examples of this system, the aggregate importance of hardwoods may be greater than pine, especially on sub xeric and mesic sites. Oden RD, Poteau/Cold Spring RD, Big Piney RD, Pleasant Hill RD, Fort Chaffee Joint Maneuver Training Center, Jamestown Independence County WMA, Gene Rush WMA, Scott Henderson Gulf Mountain WMA Spatial Ecology Narrative Historically, this habitat type occured mostly in the Ouachitas as closed canopy forest or open woodland in all aspects, where short-leaf pine was dominant. It also occured in the southeastern Ozarks and the Arkansas River Valley on acidic, sandstone soils. Today, landscape level examples do occur in conservation ownership, though most examples on private land are lost or severely degraded. Conversion has occured for pasture and pine plantation on a large scale and urbanization on a smaller scale. Remaining, but unmanaged examples are typically degraded from densification and mesophicatoin and, potentially, high grading. These examples are now closed canopy forest, though understory seedbanks typically remain intact and are restorable with silvicultural practices and fire. Landscape-level connectivity is very good on conserved land. Although degraded remanants are common and well connected on private land connection of healthy (grassland state) examples is poor. Goal Work with private landowners through USDA funding programs to increase grassland connectivity on a landscape scale. lands typically meet desired conditions, though examples on private land are typically degraded. For the latter, restoration is possible due to intact seedbanks. Silvicultural methods needed for restoration can often be self-funded through commercial timber harvest, if done correctly. For examples converted to pine plantation, restoration to a functional facsimile is also possible. For examples converted to pasture, restoration would be difficult. Goal Continue to maintain conserved examples through natural fire regime and seek opportunities for restoration on private lands through USDA funding programs. Work with forestry industry to balance restoring natural condition and timber production. This system is composed of Short-leaf Pine dominated woodlands with open canopy and abundant herbaceous ground cover with few hardwoods among dominant canopy trees. This habitat type is usually associated with gently west slopes on west and south aspects. Fire is important to maintaining these communities. Because this system occurs in large, undissected blocks, fire is more common than in most woodland communities and the canopy is more open and the herbaceous ground cover more dense (Foti et al. 2015). Much of these historical acres have actively been converted to loblolly pine plantations or passively grown into mixed pine-hardwood forest. For the former, restoration of a facsimile is still possible through harvest and fire. For the latter, full restoration is still possible through the same means. habitat type occured mostly in the Ouachitas on moderate south and west slopes on east-west running ridges where fire could spread in large, unbroken compartments. In the Ozarks and Arkansas River Valley, it was more rare and in smaller compartments, but did occur where these conditions occured over standstone substrate. Today, landscape level examples do occur in conservation ownership, though most examples on private land are lost or severely degraded. Conversion has occured for pasture and pine plantation on a large scale and urbanization on a smaller scale. Remaining, but unmanaged examples are typically degraded from densification and mesophicatoin and, potentially, high grading. These examples are now closed canopy forest, though understory seedbanks typically remain intact and are restorable with silvicultural practices and fire. Landscape-level connectivity is very good on conserved land. Although degraded remanants are common and well connected on private land connection of healthy (grassland state) examples is poor. Goal Work with private landowners through USDA funding programs to increase grassland connectivity on a landscape scale. This system of prairies and associated savannas is found in the Arkansas River Valley and Ozarks. The Arkansas Valley is characterized by broad, level to gently rolling uplands derived from shales and is much less rugged and more heavily impacted by Arkansas River erosional processes than the adjacent mountainous regions. In addition, the valley receives annual precipitation total of 2-6 inches less than the surrounding regions due to a rain shadow produced by a combination of prevailing western winds and mountain orographic effects. The shale-derived soils associated with the Arkansas Valley prairies are thin and droughty. The prairies of the Ozark Highlands occur on level to gently rolling areas underlain by limestone and chert and soils are also thin and droughty. The combined effect of droughty soils, reduced precipitation and prevailing level topography create conditions highly conducive to the ignition and spread of fires. Prairies are typically dominated by Big Bluestem, Indian Grass, Switch Grass and Little Bluestem and a high diversity of grasses and forbs and relatively few woody plants. Savannas occur on gentle to steep slopes and are typically dominated by Post Oak and Blackjack Oak, often fairly widespread with a typical prairie herbaceous groundlayer between the trees. These grasslands are not temporary "early successional" stages, but rather late climax grasslands that have existed since the Pleistocene.*

This system was historically woodland in structure, composition and process but now includes areas of more closed canopy. ## Goal

Protect high-quality remnants via fee-title acquisitions or easements, focusing on increasing connectivity and on expanding or connecting protected areas. Small remnants in urban areas, especially Northwest Arkansas, should also be prioritized. Additional work is also needed to map out the location of remnants and to further prioritize areas for restoration and conservation. ## Narrative

The remaining fragments and remnants of this habitat type range from high-quality sites to extremely degraded or converted sites that may require extensive work to restore and maintain. Furthermore, the remaining habitat is still threatened today, primarily by urban development and agriculture (i.e., livestock grazing, hay production). Loss due to pine plantation establishment and woody encroachment is less of a threat today than it was previously, as conversion has slowed and many restorable examples exist under forest canopies. While much of this habitat type has already been lost or degraded and some threats still remain, much work has and can still be done to balance production and habitat quality on working lands, as well as continuing to convey the importance of protecting and conserving these areas. Protected areas should be managed to promote biodiversity and resiliency and to control invasive species and woody encroachment by restoring the historic fire regime and with mechanical and chemical treatment methods, when necessary. Special care should be taken to preserve the remaining biodiversity by using targeted and/or limited herbicide applications. If necessary, supplemental seeding or planting of locally-sourced genotypes may be utilized to restore biodiversity at severely degraded sites. Where restoration efforts are not possible, recreations and facsimiles may serve as an acceptable alternative to increase habitat availability and connectivity across the landscape.

## Goal

Restore and manage protected areas to increase the quality of remaining available habitat on the landscape. Special efforts should be made to maintain high-quality sites. Also, work with private landowners to restore habitat on private lands and to implement BMPs and native grazing and haying initiatives; exploring opportunities to restore or create facsimiles in current pastures, hay meadows, hardwood and cedar stands and pine 

## Narrative

Small rivers and streams comprise an estimated 18.1% of stream kilometers in Arkansas, a total of 26,110 km. They are both source environments for downstream aquatic habitats and influenced by the headwaters above them.

## Goal

Restore natural flow regimes and connectivity to lateral habitats to improve ecological function.

## Habitat Condition

## Narrative

Habitat has been degraded on small rivers due to a host of issues. This widely includes human development near and within the floodplain, the creation of road-stream barriers, and unstable seasonal weather patterns. Human development near and within the floodplain (which encompasses agriculture, housing development, logging, gravel mining, and general land clearing) often damages the riparian vegetation that small rivers require to maintain adequate stability and temperature buffering. Road-stream crossings can alter the sediment transport capacity of a small river and can serve as barriers for aquatic organisms that require movement into different sections of rivers and their tributaries. This habitat fragmentation can negatively affect the movement of aquatic organisms. The spatial distribution of organisms and habitats is also influenced by stream flow regime, which includes the patterns of water discharge, timing, and variability over time, and impacts how and where aquatic species interact with their environment. Clearing or removing riparian buffer zones around small rivers can regularly lead to severe streambank erosion. Streambank erosion has multiple negative impacts on rivers including degradation of water quality and aquatic habitat by releasing sediment into the water and causing rivers to become wider and shallower. Wider, shallower rivers make it more difficult for aquatic organisms to take refuge from extreme temperatures. Current habitat on small rivers can range from excellent to poor.

## Goal

Protect high-quality river segments focusing on increasing connectivity and on expanding or connecting protected areas. Work with partners and land owners to restore the natural pattern, profile, and dimensionsof degraded small river segments, thereby improving their ecological function. Work toward educating the public about the importance of proper riparian maintenence and stream connectivity. Work with landowners to co,plete streambank stabilization on small rivers throughout the state, especially in Conservation Opportunity Areas and ecologically sensitive reaches. In addition to providing stabilization, work towards being able to identify reaches of river that are both particularly vulnerable to habitat degradation and areas that are actively degrading. Work toward educating the public about the importance of proper riparian maintenence and stream connectivity. 

## Goal

Protect high-quality remnants via fee-title acquisitions or easements, focusing on increasing connectivity and on expanding or connecting protected areas. Additional work is also needed to map out the location of remnants and to further prioritize areas for restoration and conservation. SECAS's Southeast Conservation Blueprint may be referenced to help identify priority habitat corridors and hubs.

## Habitat Condition

## Narrative

The remaining fragments and remnants of this habitat type range from high-quality sites to extremely degraded or converted sites that may require extensive work to restore and maintain. Furthermore, the remaining habitat is still threatened today, primarily by woody encroachment and agriculture (i.e., livestock grazing, hay production). While much of this habitat type has already been lost or degraded and some threats still remain, much work has and can still be done to balance production and habitat quality on working lands, as well as continuing to convey the importance of protecting and conserving these areas. Protected areas should be managed to promote biodiversity and resiliency and to control invasive species and woody encroachment by restoring the historic fire regime and with mechanical and chemical treatment methods, when necessary. Special care should be taken to preserve the remaining biodiversity by using targeted and/or limited herbicide applications. If necessary, supplemental seeding or planting of locally-sourced genotypes may be utilized to restore biodiversity at severely degraded sites. Where restoration efforts are not possible, recreations and facsimiles may serve as an acceptable alternative to increase habitat availability and connectivity across the landscape.

## Goal

Restore and manage protected areas to increase the quality of remaining available habitat on the landscape. Special efforts should be made to maintain high-quality sites. Also, work with private landowners to restore habitat on private lands and to implement BMPs and native grazing and haying initiatives; exploring opportunities to restore or create facsimiles in current pastures, hay meadows, hardwood and cedar stands and pine plantations. This system represents flatwoods found on Pleistocene high terraces, typically outside the floodplain. Soils are fine-textured and hardpans may be present in the subsurface. The limited permeability of these soils contributes to shallowly perched water tables during portions of the year when precipitation is greatest and evapotranspiration is lowest. Soil moisture fluctuates widely throughout the growing season, from saturated to very dry, a condition sometimes referred to as hydroxeric moisture regime. Saturation is primarily influenced by precipitation rather than overbank flooding. Loblolly Pine is codominant along with Cherrybark Oak and Willow Oak, with a graminoid-rich groundlayer. Approximately twenty percent of the system is occupied by prairie mounds with Short-leaf Pine, Vaccinium spp. and Sweetleaf. Extremely dry seasonal conditions make fire an important natural process in the system. As a result, this system was typically a woodland, although recent fire suppression and forest management have caused a conversion of most sites to forest. 

## Narrative

Examples on conserved lands are, in general, at or near desired conditions. Much of this acreage is intensively managed for red-cockaded woodpeckers. Most examples on private lands have been converted to pine plantation and some to pasture. It is likely that ripping for pine plantations has broken the hardpan that historically made these habitats unique. In areas where this is the case, restoration to this habitat type may not be possible.

## Goal

Protect sites where this habitat occurs and maintain desired vegetation conditions, particularly protecting the herbaceous layer and the hardpan.
West Habitat Description
This ecological system consists of forests and woodlands dominated by Loblolly Pine and/or Short-leaf Pine in combination with a host of dry to drymesic site hardwood species. This type was the historical matrix (dominant vegetation type) within this region. This habitat was historically present on nearly all uplands in the region except on the most edaphically limited sites (droughty sands, calcareous clays and shallow soil barrens/rock outcrops). Such sites are underlain by loamy to fine-textured soils of variable depths. These are upland sites on ridgetops and adjacent side slopes, with moderate fertility and moisture retention. There are no known "fidel" herbaceous species or any local endemic or globally rare plant species and overall this system may have supported relatively low levels of vascular plant species diversity. This system has undergone major transformations since European settlement and has been largely converted to cultivated pine plantations and other human uses. In limited upland areas, especially side slopes and ravines, mesic hardwood forests occur within this matrix. These areas were somewhat protected topographically from historically fire-prone, pinedominated uplands. Sites are often found along slopes above perennial or intermittent streams in the region. 

## Narrative

Despite this habitat type being the matrix type in the Gulf Coastal Plain, it is not well represented in conservation ownership. On private lands, the vast majority has been converted to pine plantation. This has not been a conservation priority since there are no known species endemic to this type, but rather a cross section of most species that occur in the Gulf Coastal Plain. Landscape connectivity is important to species such as black bear, pointing out the importance of landscape management of this type, where possible. Doing so would require balancing timber management goals on industrial ownership.

## Goal

Work 

## Narrative

The narrow geography of this habitat type makes it a conservation priority, only occuring along within the floodplain of the Red River. Some parts (mostly the higher elevations with less frequent flooding) have been converted to agriculture, pasture and/or pine plantation, the lower elevations are mostly intact. However, most acreage occurs on private (some of which industrial) ownership, with few acres in conservation ownership. Landscape connectivity is good for now, but not secure long term.

## Goal

Fee title ownership and conservation easements are desired. Habitat Condition Narrative Higher elevations often degraded, but lower elevations generally intact. Threats include high grading, conversion to agriculture, pasture and plantation and water pollution in the short term. Long term, hydrologic changes including levees and channelization may result in un-seasonal flooding (longer depth and duration) and/or an end in river meandering. 

## Narrative

This is among the most restrictive and threatened habitats in the state and occurs on specific soil types high in saline content, throughout the Coastal Plain, but concentrated in it's northern limits in Central Arkansas. Distribution is patchy and naturally isolated, similar to glades elsewhere in the state. They are generally intact, though degraded, where they occur. However, many examples have been lost due to mining. These barrens are often misunderstood by landowners who are unaware of their conservation importance.

## Goal

Fee title ownership and conservation easements are high priority.
Cooperative management with industrial owners is also an opportunity to increase landscape connectivity.

## Habitat Condition

## Narrative

Degraded examples are typically evident by poor pine growth, often after repeated loblolly planting. Many are impacted by road construction, vehicle use and minig. Due to the extreme nature of these soils, regular maintenance is typically not needed, other than fire in the grassland ring around barrens. Protection is typically all that is needed. 

## Goal

## Narrative

This habitat type occurs as pockets of forested wetland within an upland forest matrix, generally not connected to other examples. Examples of this system of forested seeps may be found on low landscape positions typically along lowgradient creeks, headwaters of drainages, or local depressions. Soils are typically semipermanently saturated, typically acidic, low nutrient and high in organic material. Many examples remain on protected lands. Even on highly modified industrial timber landscapes, these seeps are often left undeveloped, though they may be degraded by surrounding land use (livestock, hydrologic alterations, roads, forestry activities, etc.).

## Goal

Continue protecting examples with existing BMPs. Habitat Condition Narrative These habitats are, for the most part, intact, but may be impacted indirectly by adjacent land use. The most siginficant moden threats are modification of recharge areas and sedimentation from nearby forestry activities. BMPs already exist to protect these areas and should be followed.

## Goal

Continue protecting examples with existing BMPs. 

## Narrative

The narrow geography of this habitat type makes it a conservation priority. Some parts (mostly the higher elevations with less frequent flooding) have been converted to agriculture, pasture and/or pine plantation, the lower elevations are mostly intact. However, most acreage occurs on private (some of which industrial) ownership, with few acres in conservation ownership. Landscape connectivity is good for now, but not secure long term.

## Goal

Fee title ownership and conservation easements are desired. Habitat Condition Narrative Higher elevations often degraded, but lower elevations generally intact. Threats include high grading, conversion to agriculture, pasture and plantation and water pollution. Can be degraded from impacts to forestry activities, when established BMPs are not followed.

## Goal

Follow existing BMPs.
West Conservation Lands: Cherokee Prairie NA (ANHC), H.E. Flanagan Prairie NA (ANHC), Presson-Oglesby Preserve (TNC), Fort Chaffee WMA (ARARNG/AGFC), Ouachita National Forest (USFS).
Habitats: This COA is dominated by upland woodlands (oak-dominated), shortleaf pine woodlands, and grasslands. In addition, AVPCOA contains 1,208 acres of mapped glades. Native tallgrass prairie plant communities include the full range of ecological conditions from dry little bluestem dominated grasslands to wet-mesic prairie dominated by sedges, rushes, and switchgrass. The AVPCOA is primarily a terrestrial focused COA, however there are several rivers and streams that are known to provide habitat for SGCN.
Species: The large footprint and variety of habitats of the AVPCOA results in a high species diversity. Grassland dependent species include crawfish frog, prairie skink, Henslow's sparrow, Smith's Longspur, American burying beetle (listed threatened), Arogos skipper, and rattlesnake master borer-moth. All federally listed bat species and three of the four federally listed plant species in Arkansas occur in the woodlands and forests here (Ozark big-eared bat, gray bat, Indiana bat, Northern long-eared bat, Eastern small-footed bat, tricolored bat, geocarpon, harperella, Missouri bladderpod). Aquatic species include little spectaclecase, suckermouth minnow, and slenderhead darter.
Threats: The habitats within the AVPCOA are negatively impacted by conversion, fragmentation, degradation by over-haying and grazing, urbanization, alteration (or no) fire regime, increasing woody stems in grasslands and densification in woodlands, and invasive and cultivated nonnative plants.
Conservation Objectives:
• Remove non-native invasive species.
• Prevent conversion and fragmentation of prairie-woodland habitats through acquisition and easement, conservation forestry practices, restoration of native communities on conserved areas and private lands. • Reintroduce fire and remove woody species to promote herbaceous species growth and enhance burning. • Increase the quality of native habitat on private lands and work to improve connectivity among managed lands. • Increase knowledge of the biodiversity and ecology of ARVCOA.

## Conservation Objectives:

• Remove barriers and reconnect existing forest blocks to rivers and allow for flooding.
• Connect large forest blocks and reduce fragmentation.
• Determine feasibility of restoring historic patterns of water flow.
• Restore streams in planted forested blocks, other forested wetlands, and adjacent agricultural fields. Threats: Blacklands were historically abundant in the south-central United States. Less than 1 percent of this habitat occurs. Conversion to agriculture, urbanization, or other land uses is the primary threat. Other threats in the BPCOA include altered fire regimes, invasive plants, conversion to non-native pasture grasses, and habitat fragmentation through development.

## Conservation Objectives:

• Prevent further conversion of blackland prairie.
• Reintroduce fire or continue its use to establish the appropriate prescribed fire regime for the blackland prairie. • Prevent and reduce impacts from terrestrial non-native invasive species.
• Prevent new soil erosion and alteration to the hydrologic regime of the Blackland ecosystem and mitigate/restore areas already affected by these processes.
COA Partners:
- Habitats: The Caddo River is designated by the state of Arkansas as both Ecologically Sensitive and Extraordinary Resource Waters. The Caddo River flows generally from west to southeast through the Ouachita National Forest. The Caddo River is typical of Ouachita Mountains streams, characterized by incised single-thread, moderate to high gradient riffle-pool systems dominated by gravel/cobble bed substrate. Springs are abundant. While the CRHCOA is primarily an aquaticfocused COA, there are several riparian and riparian-adjacent terrestrial natural communities that are either of conservation concern themselves or support species of conservation concern. These communities include 1) Riverscour/High Gradient Stream Channel, 2) Channel Scar Depression Wetlands, 3) Mesic Forested River Terrace, 4) Seeps and Springs, 5) Cliffs and Talus, and 6) Shale and Novaculite Glades and Barrens.
Species: The Caddo River Watershed supports the conservation of the exceptional aquatic biodiversity of global significance found in the Ouachita Mountains. The CRHCOA includes ecologically important habitats for two federally listed species and 55 other species of greatest conservation need (SGCN). Notably this watershed supports species endemic to the Ouachita Mountains and found nowhere else in the world, including Paleback Darter, Beaded Darter, Caddo Matom, Redspotted Stream Crayfish and others.
Threats: The waters within the CRHCOA are negatively impacted by loss of riparian forest, increased sediment, alteration and degradation of habitats from development, and increased pollutants in streams. Many of these threats have legacy origins in early and midterm settlement times. Gravel mining has also been a common practice. The rivers and streams are incised and transporting considerable excess sediment.
Conservation Objectives:
• Protect and restore floodplain/riparian forests.
• Enable zoning requirements that protect floodplains.
• Assess sediment sources and transform sediment abatement strategies.
• Reduce sedimentation from eroding stream banks by implementing stabilization projects in high priority river segments. • Improve water quality and reduce hydrological and geomorphic effects of development on aquatic and terrestrial communities by implementing projects aimed at mitigating impacts.
• Set target concentrations that will reduce nitrogen and phosphorus levels in sewage outflow from municipal sources. • Identify important breeding areas for paleback darters and prioritize these areas for protection. • Improve aquatic connectivity and fish passage by removing targeted barriers.
• Prevent and reduce impacts of cattle and other livestock in streams.
• Maintain natural flow regime.
• Restore and maintain forest cover at 80% or more of the Caddo River watershed.
• Reduce habitat degradation on rights of way by implementing improved management practices. • Prevent and reduce impacts from non-native aquatic species. Habitats: CPSWCOA is dominated by upland woodlands, oak and oak-shortleaf pine and grasslands. Native tallgrass prairie and savanna plant communities in the CPSWCOA include the full range of ecological conditions from dry little bluestem dominated grasslands to wet-mesic prairie dominated by sedges, rushes, and switchgrass. Native oak-dominated woodlands are widespread in the CPSWCOA and form the matrix habitat. Embedded within this matrix are sandstone and shale glades. Aquatic habitats include the Arkansas River, small creeks, and forested, shrub, and herbaceous wetlands.
Species: There are many grassland obligate species in the COA, including Diana fritillary, crawfish frog, prairie skink, Northern bobwhite, and Bush's poppy-mallow. Aquatic species include alligator gar, American eel, and chicken turtle.
Threats: The habitats within the CPSWCOA are negatively impacted by lack of fire management, conversion and fragmentation of habitat, increasing woody stems in grasslands and densification in woodlands, degradation by over-haying and grazing, and invasive and cultivated non-native plants.
Conservation Objectives:
• In conservation areas (private and public lands), reintroduce fire and mechanically remove woody species where necessary to promote herbaceous species growth and enhance burning. • Prevent conversion and fragmentation of prairie-woodland habitats through acquisition and easement, conservation forestry and grazing practices, and restoration of native communities on conserved areas and private lands. Conservation Lands: Beaver Lake (USACE), Bull Shoals Lake (USACE), Devil's Knob/Devil's Backbone NA (ANHC), Jones Point WMA (AGFC), Norfolk Lake (USACE), Slippery Hollow NA (ANHC), Table Rock Lake (USACE), Sylamore WMA (USFS, AGFC)
Habitats: The GWCOA is dominated by Upland Hardwood Forest widely interspersed with grassland vegetation. The GWCOA contains 21,141 acres of mapped glades, which is 58% of the total area of mapped glades within COAs. The GWCOA is entirely within the White River Watershed which includes three large reservoirs on the mainstem. Although well forested at over 60% of the landcover, 31% of the landcover is altered with 18% in pasture and 6% urban. Floodplains have been cleared for pastures, as well as accommodations for large concentrations of poultry and livestock, thus, the rivers carry high nutrient and sediment loads. Subterranean (karst) habitats in the GWCOA come in various sizes, forms, and types and include features such as caves, losing/gaining stream sections, springs, and seeps.
Species: The glade and woodland ecosystem provides habitat for 4 federally listed bat species (Gray Bat, Indiana Bat, Northern Long-eared Bat, Ozark Big-eared Bat) and forty-five Species of Greatest Conservation Need (SGCN). Two federally listed species are found in the aquatic and karst systems (Hell Creek Cave Crayfish and Snuffbox mussel). Many rare plants are present including Trelease's Larkspur, Yellow Coneflower, and Royal Catchfly.
Threats: A wide range of threats to the ecological integrity of glade habitats have been identified for the GWCOA. These threats include woody densification (mostly eastern red cedar), fire suppression, non-native invasive species, disturbance from recreational use and management activities, development, and climate change.
Conservation Objectives:
• Mechanically remove eastern red cedar and other woody species to promote herbaceous species growth and enhance the effectiveness of prescribed fire maintenance. • Remove non-native invasive species.
• Restore fire regimes, prioritizing glade complexes where fire can be maintained over time.
• Develop a response to the uncertainty around climate change and its impact. Species: The Little River Basin supports a diverse aquatic community with twenty-two aquatic species of greatest conservation need (SGCN), including four federally listed mussels (Ouachita rock pocketbook, pink mucket, scaleshell, winged mapleleaf). This area is crucially important for the federally listed leopard darter.
Threats: The lands and waters within the LRBCOA are negatively impacted by alteration and degradation of habitats from the construction of mainstem dams, second home development, land management practices, and increased pollutants and sediment in streams. Two mainstem dams were built on the Cossatot and Rolling Fork rivers, and the Little River has been impacted by mainstem and tributary dams in Oklahoma.
Conservation Objectives:
• Modify dam operations to better imitate historical flows and water temperatures.
• Protect and restore floodplain/riparian forests.
• Reduce sedimentation from unpaved roads by implementing improved management practices on high priority unpaved road segments. • Improve fish passage by removing/remediating barriers.
• Reduce sedimentation from eroding streambanks by implementing stabilization projects in high priority river segments. • Reduce the use of herbicides on rights-of-way by implementing improved management practices.
• Prevent and reduce impacts from non-native aquatic species.
• Increase knowledge of location and conservation needs of seeps, springs, and seasonal wetlands.
COA Partners:
- Species: Over 50 SGCN are known from the COA, many of them aquatic. This includes five federally listed mussel species (Western fanshell, snuffbox, scaleshell, pink mucket). The federally endangered gray bat is also present.
Threats: The lands and waters within the upland portion of the LSMBCOA are negatively impacted by alteration and degradation of riparian habitats from land management practices, barriers to movement in the rivers, and increased pollutants and sediment in streams. Many of these threats have legacy origins in early and midterm settlement times. Climate change exacerbates the impact of all other threats. Although total stream flows appear stable, the threat from climate change is evident in longer periods of low water and likely higher water temperatures, as well as higher and more frequent high flow events.
Conservation Objectives:
• Protect and restore floodplain and riparian forests.
• Prevent and reduce impacts of cattle and other livestock in streams.
• Reduce sedimentation from eroding streambanks by implementing stabilization projects in high priority river segments. Threats: The lands and waters within the MEPRCOA are negatively impacted by alteration and degradation of riparian habitats from land management practices, barriers to movement in the rivers, and increased pollutants and sediment in streams. Many of these threats have legacy origins in early and midterm settlement times. Climate change exacerbates the impact of all other threats. Although total stream flows appear stable, the threat from climate change is evident in longer periods of low water and likely higher water temperatures, as well as higher and more frequent high flow events.
Conservation Objectives:
• Protect and restore floodplain and riparian forests.
• Prevent and reduce impacts of cattle and other livestock in streams.
• Reduce sedimentation from eroding stream banks by implementing stabilization projects in high priority river segments. Historically the vegetation matrix in Northwest Arkansas was tallgrass prairie and oak savanna. Currently, there are fewer than 200 acres of high-quality tallgrass prairie remaining. The oakdominated woodlands and forests are mostly fire-suppressed and dense but still largely extant and restorable. There are 208 acres of mapped glades mostly embedded within the oak woodlands.
Species: The NKARCOA contains an underground diversity that is still being cataloged. There are 81 Species of Greatest Conservation Need (SGCN) across a range of taxonomic groups. Nine SGCN species are cave/karst obligates. There are several federally listed species (Indiana bat, gray bat, Northern long-eared bat, Ozark big-eared bat, Ozark cavefish, Benton County cave crayfish).
Threats: The habitats in the Northwest Karst Arkansas River COA are most threatened by landcover conversion and infrastructure installed for rapidly urbanizing Washington and Benton counties. As much as 67% of the land area has been converted to non-native cover (urban and fescue pasture).
Conservation Objectives:
• Prevent conversion and fragmentation of terrestrial, karst, and aquatic habitats through acquisition of fee-title and easement of priority lands, best management practices, mitigation, and restoration of native habitats. • Conserve and restore floodplain and riparian forests.
• Improve water quality and reduce hydrological and geomorphic effects of urbanization on aquatic habitats. • Ensure caves and other karst features used by bats are protected from disturbance.
• Maintain and improve water quality in groundwater and springs.
• Prevent and reduce impacts of cattle and other livestock in streams.
• Reduce habitat degradation on rights-of-way by implementing improved management practices. • Protect groundwater from overuse.
• Reduce the use of herbicides on rights-of-way by implementing improved management practices. • Reduce impacts of current non-native species and prepare to react to new invasions.
