[FIGURE: An ecologically functioning, connected woodland-grassland habitat matrix that provides for the needs of the full range of species of greatest conservation need. ____________________________________________________________________________ Location: The Central Prairie Savanna Woodland Conservation Opportunity Area (CPSWCOA) comprises 303,722 acres in central Arkansas in Pulaski, Faulkner, Conway, and Perry counties. Located in the Arkansas Valley and eastern Ouachita Mountains ecoregions, CPSWCOA includes the eastern extension of the rugged Fourche Mountains, the alluvial floodplain of the Arkansas River, the flat Arkansas Valley Plains, and scattered Arkansas Valley High Ridges and Hills. Conservation Lands: Bell Slough WMA, Camp Robinson Special Use Area (AGFC), Camp Robinson WMA (ARARNG), Stone Prairie WMA (AGFC).]

[FIGURE: A system of ecologically functioning glade and woodland habitat complexes at a scale that will sustain species of conservation concern. _____________________________________________________________________________ Location: The Glades and Woodlands Conservation Opportunity Area (GWCOA) totals 710,778 acres entirely within the Ozarks Highlands Ecoregion; it stretches across Northern Arkansas in three separate blocks. It includes portions of eight counties: Benton, Carroll, Boone, Fulton, Stone, Izard, Marion, and Baxter.]

[FIGURE: Conservation Lands: H.E. Alexander Spring River WMA (AGFC), Rock Creek NA (ANHC) Habitats: The lower portion of the Spring River flows for approximately 35 miles before joining the Black River on the MAV. There are 538 miles of stream, of which 414 miles are classified as 1st and 2nd order with 31 miles classified as 8th and 9th order. Most tributaries are losing, with water flowing into the karst aquifer and/or are ephemeral. Outflow from the karst aquifer forms caves, springs, and spring runs. The Spring River and its major tributaries are identified as Ecologically Sensitive Waterbodies (ESW). Important upland terrestrial habitats are: riparian, seeps, calcareous glades and woodlands, mesic oak forest, and mixed pine-hardwood forests. Important lowland habitats include riparian/floodplain forests, forested wetlands, and herbaceous and shrub wetlands.]

[FIGURE: Conservation Lands: Cave Springs NA (ANHC), Garrett Hollow NA (ANHC), Healing Springs NA (ANHC), Logan Cave NWR (USFWS), Logan Springs Preserve (TNC), Ozark Plateau NWR (USFWS), Ozark National Forest (USFS), Pea Ridge National Military Park (NPS), Searles Prairie NA (ANHC), Wedington WMA (AGFC).Habitats: Subterranean habitats in NKARCOA come in various sizes, forms, and types and include features such as caves, bluff lines with cracks and crevices in them, losing/gaining stream sections, springs, and seeps. The NKARCOA is almost entirely within the Illinois River Watershed with a northern extension into the upper portion of the Sugar Creek which flows north into Missouri. There are 2,735 miles of stream of which 1,619 miles are classified as first order.]

* historically occurred as pockets of prairie within a pine woodland landscape in a small geographic area the West Gulf Coastal Plain in southwestern Arkansas. It is restricted to high pH calcium deposits, remnants of shallow Cretaceous seas that today display as chalk deposits and conspicuous marine fossils embedded in rich, black soil. Similar blackland prairies occur elsewhere in the Southeastern United States, though this area of Arkansas contains the largest and most pristine examples. Today, the majority of historic acreage has been lost or degraded due to fire suppression and overgrazing and agriculture. At the time of European settlement, these prairies were among the first settled. Across the southeast, over 90% of native grasslands have been lost since European colonization. Although large conserved examples exist, connectivity across the landscape is poor. Degraded examples are either encroached by Eastern Red Cedar or intensively grazed. Although embedded in a pine matrix, few examples have been successfully converted to pine plantation due to unconducive soils for pine.*

* toward landscape connectivity in the Gulf Coastal Plain, working across conservation ownership, industrial ownership and non-industrial ownership.This system is restricted to the main stem of the Red River in southwestern Arkansas. Several distinct plant communities can be recognized within this system that may be related to the array of different geomorphic features present within the floodplain. Some of the major geomorphic features associated with different community types within the system include natural levees, point bars, meander scrolls, oxbows and sloughs. Vegetation generally includes forests dominated by bottomland hardwood species, with sites ranging from relatively dry to cypress-tupelo swamps. This system is generally similar in concept to West Gulf Coastal Plain Large River Floodplain Forest but is distinct from it because of the difference in magnitude between the typical large rivers (Ouachita, Saline) and the Red River bottoms. Native vegetation in the Red River bottoms differs from that of the West Gulf Coastal Plain Large River Floodplain Forest in having a larger area occupied by Eastern Cottonwood, Black Willow and other sandy riverfront forests.*

* GoalWork with US Army Corps of Engineers on mitigating environmental damage from future projects and work with landowners on best management practices for agriculture and forestry.Saline barrens are rare patch communities on the Upper West Gulf Coastal Plain. The soil has saline and magnesium content that is lethal to most plants.Hence, the number of rare and unusual plants that are found in saline barrens. Saline barrens exist as open glades in woodland complexes, grading into low productivity pine and pine-oak flatwoods as the soil becomes less lethal. In their severest form they are not conducive to woody plant invasion even under fire suppression. Bare soil is usually present. Saline barrens also occur in the Arkansas Valley and Mississippi Alluvial Plain.*

* Continue restoration of examples on conserved lands and work with industry partners to develop BMPs on industrial ownership.This habitat occurs on uplands underlain by deep, coarse sandy soils. These sites are typified by low fertility and moisture retention, which contribute to open tree canopies with usually <60% canopy closure. Sparse understory vegetation and patches of bare soil are indicative of this system. Vegetation indicators are species tolerant of droughty sites, especially Bluejack Oak and Arkansas Oak. Short-leaf Pine is usually present. Loblolly Pine is naturally less common, but in the current landscape, it is common and sometimes dominant. This habitat may be essentially treeless sand barrens. Fire is a critical natural disturbance process which affects the vegetation structure and likely the species composition of communities in this system. This system supports a large concentration of vascular plant endemics, near endemics and a number of plant species with high fidelity to sandhills in the region. the most restrictive and threatened habitats in the state and occurs on specific soil types on ridges with deep, coarse, sandy soil in southwest Arkansas. Distribution is patchy and naturally isolated, similar to glades elsewhere in the state. They are generally intact, though degraded, where they occur. Being an isolated grassland habitat type in a forested matrix, connectivity to other sandhill patches is dependent on grassland connectivity through surrounding woodlands. As such, management of surrounding habitats in relatively open, grassy conditions may allow species to migrate between sandhill patches. There are several good examples in conservation ownership, though much work of aqcuiring other examples is still to be done. Degraded examples are typically evident by poor pine growth, often after repeated loblolly planting. Due to extreme soil conditions, degraded examples are typically restorable with tree removal and reintroduction of fire. Examples in conservation ownership are, in general, at or on a path toward DFCs.*

* • Conserve glade-gladey woodland complexes to improve and maintain habitat connectivity.• Develop agency and community engagement programs that provide information on the potential impact of disturbances and mitigation options. The Little River Basin Conservation Opportunity Area (LRBCOA) totals 643,251 acres and is located in southwestern Arkansas, covering portions of Sevier, Howard, and Polk counties adjacent to the Oklahoma border. The southern portion of the LRBCOA lies in the West Gulf Coastal ecoregion stretching north into the Ouachita Mountains ecoregion. The LRBCOA includes ecologically important river systems within five 8-digit HUCS: Headwaters Cossatot, Upper Little River, Middle Little River, Upper Cossatot River, Lower Cossatot River, and a portion of the Little River on the north bank (Pond Creek) until it flows into Lake Millwood. The major tributaries to the Little River in Arkansas are the Rolling Fork and the Cossatot River. The Little River Bottoms (Pond Creek), the floodplain and bottomland hardwood forest north of the Little River, covers 66,700 acres.*

## Appendix C: COA Conservation Plans Overviews

The AGFC and conservation partners developed conservation plans for Arkansas's 19 conservation opportunity areas. These detailed plans identify important habitats, species of greatest conservation need, threats, and conservation actions within the COA. A summary of each of the conservation plans is provided here. Full conservation plans are available from AGFC.

## Big Woods Conservation Opportunity Area

Conservation Plan Overview Habitats: The BWCOA is a terrestrial focused COA and includes extensive forested wetlands and areas that have been replanted to forest. Mature forests that have never been converted cover approximately 240,000 acres. Habitats include backswamps, hardwood and pine flatwoods, oxbow lakes, riparian forests, terrace forests, marshes, shrub swamps, and remnant high sandbar grasslands, a grassland type just being formally described. The lower White-Cache River and lower Arkansas River systems provide habitat for aquatic SGCN.
Species: Over 60 SGCN are found in the Big Woods COA, including four federally listed species: Red-cockaded woodpecker, pink mucket, rabbitsfoot, and pallid sturgeon. Several marsh birds of concern occur here, including American bittern, least bittern, king rail, common gallinule, and purple gallinule.
Threats: The habitats within the BWCOA are negatively impacted by altered hydrologic processes, disconnection of forests from floodplains, fragmentation of forest habitat, non-point Habitats: The NKWRCOA features karst, aquatic, and terrestrial habitats. Subterranean habitats in NKWRCOA come in various sizes, forms, and types and include features such as caves, bluff lines with cracks and crevices in them, losing/gaining stream sections, springs, and seeps. The NKWRCOA is almost entirely within the West Fork of the White River Watershed. The West Fork flows into the main stem of the White River and then Beaver Lake, the southern portion of which is included in the NKWRCOA. Priority terrestrial habitats are riparian forests, canebrakes, tallgrass and wet prairie, glades and gladey woodlands, and oak woodlands.
Species: The NKWRCOA contains an underground diversity that is still being catalogued. There are 67 SGCN across a range of taxonomic groups. The NKWRCOA contains habitat for five federally listed species (gray bat, Indiana bat, Northern long-eared bat, Missouri bladderpod, and Ozark cavefish). The Ozark shiner and tricolored bat, both under review for listing, have also been reported from the area.

## Ouachita River Headwaters Conservation Opportunity Area

Conservation Plan Overview ___________________________________________________________________________ Conservation Vision: A system of ecologically functioning rivers and streams with intact, riparian buffers that sustain healthy aquatic communities. Prioritized habitats for SBPFCOA are 1) saline barrens, 2) pine flatwood woodlands, 3) nonforested wetlands, 4) bottomland forest and associated wetlands, and 5) upland mixed pine-oak woodlands.
The SBPFCOA includes the lower Saline and Ouachita rivers and their bottomlands. Important aquatic habitats are: small rivers, non-forested wetlands, and oxbows.
Species: There are more than 50 SGCN in the SBPFCOA, including five federally listed species (pink mucket, winged mapleleaf, rabbitsfoot, red-cockaded woodpecker, and geocarpon).
Threats: Threats in the SBPFCOA include altered fire regime, woody densification, fragmentation and conversion of habitat, development, forestry practices, and non-native invasive species.

## Sandhills Conservation Opportunity Area

Conservation Plan Overview ___________________________________________________________________________ Conservation Vision: A system of ecologically functioning sandhill woodland and sand barren habitat complexes at a scale that will sustain species of conservation concern. Habitats: The SHCOA is dominated by dry sand oak woodlands and sand barrens on droughty sandy soils, other shortleaf pine -oak woodlands, and cultivated pine plantation. Sand barrens and wooded grasslands are perhaps the richest in plant species diversity in Arkansas. The sand barrens are relatively small patches from 1 -40 acres in size, within an oak woodland that covered 100,000 acres or more historically. The barrens are dominated by drought resistant grasses, lichens, mosses, and bare ground with scattered forbs. Other important habitats include seeps, baygalls, and marshes. Riparian and bottomland forests are found along creeks and rivers. Large, forested bottoms and associated open wetlands are found along the Sulphur and Red rivers.
Species Habitats: The Strawberry River flows for approximately 115, free-flowing, miles before joining the Black River on the MAV. There are 538 miles of stream of which 414 miles are classified as 1st and 2nd order with 31 miles classified as 8th and 9th order. The Strawberry River is part of the Arkansas Natural and Scenic Rivers system. Priority aquatic habitats are 1) small rivers, 2) large river, 3) wetlands, and 4) groundwater. Subterranean (karst) habitats in SRCOA come in various sizes, forms, and types and include features such as caves, bluff lines with cracks and crevices in them, losing/gaining stream sections, springs, and seeps.
Important terrestrial upland habitats are 1) Riparian and mesic river terraces, 2) Seeps, fens, and springs, 3) Calcareous glades, 4) Upland calcareous woodlands, and 5)mesic oak forest. Lowland habitats include riparian forests, forested wetlands, and herbaceous wetlands.
Species: The SRCOA provides habitat for 30 aquatic SGCN, including 4 federally listed mussel species (Western fanshell, snuffbox, scaleshell, and rabbitsfoot). The endangered gray bat also occurs here. Glades offer habitat for the eastern collared lizard and the federally endangered Missouri bladderpod plant.
Threats: The lands and waters within the upland portion of the SRCOA are negatively impacted by alteration and degradation of riparian habitats from land management practices, increased sediment in streams, and increased nutrient loads. Many of these threats have legacy origins in early and midterm settlement times. Environmental change (e.g., temperature, drought, precipitation) exacerbates the impact of all other threats. Although total stream flows appear stable, the threat from environmental change is evident in longer periods of low water and likely higher water temperatures, as well as higher and more frequent high flow events.
Conservation Objectives:
• Protect and restore floodplain and riparian forests.
• Reduce sedimentation from unpaved roads by implementing improved management practices on priority unpaved road segments. • Reduce sedimentation from eroding streambanks by implementing stabilization projects in high priority river segments. • Prevent and reduce impacts of cattle and other livestock in streams.
• Improve fish passage by removing targeted barriers.
• Prevent and reduce impacts from non-native aquatic species.
• Restore and maintain forest cover to 80% of the SRCOA. Restore native woodland conditions on 5,000 acres that historically would have been woodland. • Improve water quality and reduce hydrological and geomorphic effects of development on aquatic and terrestrial communities by implementing one project aimed at mitigating impacts. • Reduce habitat degradation on rights-of-way by implementing improved management practices. • Protect and restore floodplain/riparian forests.
• Remove barriers and reconnect existing forest blocks to rivers and allow for flooding.
• Restore streams in planted forested blocks, other forested wetlands, and adjacent agricultural fields.
COA Partners:
- Conservation Lands: Bluffton Preserve (TNC), Greers Ferry Lake (USACE), Gulf Mountain WMA (AGFC), Middle Fork Preserve (TNC), South Fork Preserve (TNC), Ozark National Forest (USFS)
Habitats: Within the ULRRCOA there are 2,248 miles of stream with 60% being 1st order ephemeral streams. There are four major forks in the ULRRCOA: South, Archey, Middle, Devil's (Beech) that flow separately into Greers Ferry Lake. All four forks are designated by the state of Arkansas as Ecologically Sensitive Waterbodies. While the ULRRCOA is primarily aquatic focused, there are several riparian and riparian-adjacent terrestrial natural communities that are either of conservation concern themselves or support species of conservation concern. These communities include 1) Riverscour/High Gradient Stream Channel, 2) Channel Scar Depression Wetlands, 3) Riparian and Mesic Forested River Terrace, 4) Seeps and Springs, 5) Cliffs and Talus, and 6) Shale and Sandstone Glades and Barrens Species: The upper Little Red River contains habitat for one of the most biodiverse aquatic communities in Arkansas. This river basin is inhabited by 55 SGCN across a range of taxonomic groups. Several of these species are narrow range endemics, including the yellowcheek darter and speckled pocketbook mussel. The federally listed Northern long-eared bat and gray bat also occur here.
Threats: The lands and waters within the ULLRCOA are negatively impacted by alteration and degradation of habitats from land management practices, barriers to movement in the rivers, and increased pollutants and sediment in streams. Climate change exacerbates the impacts of all other threats. Although total stream flows appear stable, the threat from climate change is evident in longer periods of low water and likely higher water temperatures, as well as higher and more frequent high flow events. Increasingly long droughts are thought to be impacting yellowcheek darter populations.
Conservation Objectives:
• Protect and restore floodplain and riparian forests.
• Assess sediment sources and transform sediment abatement strategies.
• Reduce sedimentation from unpaved roads by implementing improved management practices on priority unpaved road segments. • Improve fish passage by removing targeted barriers.
• Prevent and reduce impacts of cattle and other livestock in streams.
• Set target concentrations that will reduce nitrogen and phosphorus levels in sewage outflow from the Leslie treatment plant. • Increase distribution, abundance, and genetic diversity of SGCN fishes.
• Restore and maintain forest cover at 80% or more of the ULRRCOA.
• Reduce habitat degradation on rights-of-way by implementing improved management practices. • Prevent and reduce impacts from non-native aquatic species. While the USRCOA is primarily an aquatic-focused COA, there are several riparian and riparianadjacent terrestrial natural communities that are either of conservation concern themselves or support species of conservation concern. These communities include 1) Riverscour/High Gradient Stream Channel, 2) Channel Scar Depression Wetlands, 3) Mesic Forested River Terrace, 4) Seeps and Springs, 5) Cliffs and Talus, and 6) Shale Glades and Barrens.
Species: The upper Saline River contains one of the most biodiverse aquatic communities in Arkansas. This river basin is inhabited by 45 Species of Greatest Conservation Need (SGCN) across a range of taxonomic groups. Several of these species are narrow range endemics, including the Redspotted Stream Crayfish, Ouachita Madtom, Caddo Sallfly, and the Arkansas Fatmucket Mussel. The USRCOA contains three federally listed mussel species and the Pyramid Pigtoe, Ouachita Fanshell, and Alligator Snapping Turtle, which are proposed for listing as threatened.
Threats: The Upper Saline River Conservation Opportunity Area (USRCOA) faces negative impacts on its lands and waters due to habitat alteration and degradation. These issues stem from urbanization, land management practices, and increased pollutants and sediment in streams.
Many of these threats have historical roots, originating in early and midterm settlement times. For example, extensive logging and wildfires affected nearly all native forests before 1920. A second clearing and conversion of forests to loblolly pine plantations occurred between the 1980s and early 2000s, leading to an extensive dirt and gravel road network. Additionally, the most productive riparian and river terraces for farming were cleared both before and after the Civil War. In some instances, rivers and creeks were even rerouted from their natural channels to create continuous farmland.
Conservation Objectives:
• Protect and restore floodplain/riparian lands.
• Reduce sedimentation from unpaved roads by implementing improved management practices on priority unpaved road segments. • Improve fish passage by removing/remediating barriers.
• Reduce sedimentation from eroding stream banks by implementing stabilization projects in high priority river segments. • Improve water quality and reduce hydrological and geomorphic effects of urbanization on aquatic and terrestrial communities. • Prevent and reduce impacts of cattle and other livestock in streams.
• Reduce habitat degradation on rights-of-way by implementing improved management practices. • Prevent and reduce impacts from non-native aquatic species.
• Work with partners to identify potential alternative water withdrawal sources.
